1. **Primary Request and Intent**

   **The project.** "Anthro" is an anthropological and biological evolution god-sim: a headless C# simulation (netstandard2.1) at `C:\Users\nars7\Downloads\Ideas and Thoughts\Anthro\`, for later use in Unity.

   **The user's core principles** (saved in memory):
   - Everything is structural, emergent and organic; no hard-coded events, schedules or global constants.
   - World facts are grounded in real data.
   - Hand-set numbers must be disclosed.

   - Don't commit to git unless asked.

   **Balance target** (refined by the user):
   - Without a god, humans persist but advance slowly.
   - Single bands of about 30 dying out is realistic.
   - The god speeds up innovation and evolution, creates story and drama, rescues people, and improves their odds.

2. **Key Technical Concepts**

   **Region and camps**
   - Region owns the world, ecology and camps. Each camp is a `Simulation` with a `Band`.
   - New `Tribe` = a people, with `Region.MaxTribes = 5`.
   - Deterministic RNG with forks; per-animal-group `Rng` allows the parallel grazer update.

   **Ecology** (closed map, no off-map pool)
   - Each `AnimalGroup` has Condition (from forage) and Pregnant (set at the autumn rut; births at day 50).
   - Population control: crowding term (1−(N/K)³) and DeathRate.
   - Wolf kills are compensatory (`Culled`). Wolves pick prey by encounter (Meat-weighted) within 4 km, hunt up to 3 times a day, and hunger is measured in weeks.
   - Migratory herds go Away (mammoths); deer migrate altitudinally within the map.
   - `Shy` (wariness of people) and `Trail` (8 daily points).
   - Tracks legible for 2 days dry, 3 in marsh, 6 in snow; `TrailsNear` spatial index.

   **Climate and cells**
   - Daily temperature curve; lapse rate 6.5 °C per km with an elevation unit of 500 m (authored).
   - Snow builds and settles; yearly rain districts (6×6 grid, AR(1)); mast, berry, grain and fish-run variation by year.
   - Cell: Temperature, Snow, Wet, Growth, SnowAccess, GroundSnow, Bark.
   - `SimClock.DayLength` follows a smooth curve.

   **Body and births**
   - `Physiology.BodyFat`.
   - Fecundity = padding (fat/0.6) × weight-trend balance × workload factor.
   - Coale–Trussell age schedule; secondary sterility.
   - Lactational suppression lasts 3–5 years depending on the mother's fat.

   **Food valuation**
   - Base pulls: room, meatRoom, huntRoom.
   - Meat craving from the hearth's `AnimalShare` (full at ≤10%, none at ≥40%).
   - Foresight from learned per-season `lean:spring` … `lean:winter`. No calendar; it adds equally to any work whose food keeps.

   **Social**
   - Learned expectations; deeds; `RelationTo` now treats camps of the same people by real relation.
   - `CampRelation` for raids.
   - Splitting driven by Overwork (Workload hours), crowding and hardship. Personal feuds move one family instead.
   - `SeekCompany`; once-a-year move cooldown; loneliness pull.
   - Demand sharing (`SpareFor`).

   **Learning**
   - Discovery spread with partial `Learning` progress; talk is capped at 40% for crafts; "heard of it" boosts reinvention.

   **Marriage and dogs**
   - Breakups via seasonal `ReconsiderMarriages` (provider gap, childlessness).
   - Dogs as animals.

   **Speed**
   - Parallel grazers; HashSet in hunt target choice; trail index.

   **Tooling**
   - Demog analysis tool in the session scratchpad: life tables, food accounting, yearly lines.
   - Args: `seed years [kinCamps] [grantList]`.
   - Runner `--kin N`; its map line shows dogs.

3. **Files and Code Sections**

   All paths are under `Anthro/Anthro.Sim/` unless stated.

   **New files**
   - **World/Climate.cs** (rewritten): MetersPerElevation 500, TemperatureOn/At, TemperatureC(season) averaged, NewYear (rain AR(1) sd 0.1/0.15, mast −0.4×log + N(0, 0.6), Berries, Grain, FishRun), Wetness(x, y, size).
   - **Ecology/EcologySystem.cs** (rewritten): Populate (0.8K), Forage, DailyUpdate (parallel `Graze` with per-group Rng; wolves sequential with `AgeAndMove`), Legible, Births (rut and pregnancy), AwayDaily, ChooseRange(sim, g, season, rng) with hungry reach of 12 km, FollowPrey (Near 4 km, meat-weighted random), PackHunts (3 attempts, Chance by flight distance, speed and condition, Culled), Index (plus the trail index), TrailsNear, Tally/Count.
   - **Dogs.cs**: DailyDogs (pups at day 60 when someone knows dog.pup and a keen animal-lover can spare food, litter of 2–4; scraps; DogsFed; breeding only above 0.4 fed; people eat dogs in famine), WolvesNear (from sightings), DogGuard.
   - **Tribe.cs**: Tribe class, Region partial (MaxTribes = 5, NewTribe, LivingTribes, CampsOf, ConsiderSchisms with rift ≥ 1 → 10%/yr scaled), Simulation partial (Tribe, LastKinContact, RiftWith, CampName "{Tribe} of {leader}").
   - **docs/AUDIT-HARDCODE.md**: categories A–G. Leaks, calendar rules and dice marked FIXED where done.

   **World and region**
   - **World/Cell.cs**: Snow, Wet, Temperature, Growth, Water, SnowAccess, GroundSnow, Bark (capacity 10, regrows 5%/yr), DailyRegrow(Climate, dayOfYear).
   - **WorldGen.cs**: forest Bark initialised.
   - **Region.cs**:
     - partial class; `Create(seed, web, bandSize = 30, otherBands = 0, kinCamps = 0)`;
     - kin camps share the founders' tribe and culture (customs, act:/tactic: expectations), with 40% cross-camp acquaintance;
     - NewWeatherYear; ConsiderSchisms in EndYear;
     - Move/MoveHouseholds call TakeDogs;
     - same-tribe meetings update LastKinContact.

   **Simulation core**
   - **Simulation.cs**:
     - CampCell and MeatKeepsDays (4 warm / 20 frozen / 60 with store.cold); LeanTopic(s);
     - temperature at the camp cell; CampAir (camp-wide contagion at 0.15 h per sick person);
     - Infection uses temperature (coldSeason, warm);
     - ChestRisk 0.003, WeaningRisk 4, AccidentRate 2.5e-6;
     - MaybeConceive uses FecundityByAge (Coale–Trussell), Sterile, and weaning 3 + 2×(1 − fat/0.8);
     - GiveBirth sets sterility with chance 0.025 + complication;
     - WeightTrend, Workload and Provided EMAs in DailyBody;
     - season-low lean learning;
     - WolvesNearCamp boldness from hunger and the strength ratio;
     - DailyDogs call; SeekCompany weekly; ReconsiderMarriages mid-season; Overwork(p) = (Workload − 7)/4.
   - **Society.cs**: SamePeople; RelationTo ignores Outsider for same-people camps; SpareFor(h, askerHearth); DoAsk refusal accounting uses SpareFor; DoAttack loserWound = Rng.Range(0.05, 0.3)×(0.5 + str).
   - **Social.cs**:
     - Needed() made static at class level;
     - ConsiderSplitting: `personal` (strife-dominant → family moves); no tiny-group founding; desperate households join known bands; strike out only if bold and not desperate; one-year cooldown since LeftDay; dogs split proportionally; message "split the camp: X led N people off to make a camp of their own";
     - SettleNew(parent, x, y, leader) sets the Tribe and camp name;
     - ConsiderJoining: shortHere × hardship × 3, alone +2, −1 for another tribe;
     - SeekCompany includes own-tribe camps;
     - discontent adds Overwork.
   - **JointActs.cs**: CampRelation; RaidValue uses rel; MeetOnSharedGround Hostility(p, o, oBand, pCamp) uses pCamp.RelationTo.
   - **Family.cs**: ConsiderLeaving(p, drawnTo, strain = 0); ReconsiderMarriages.
   - **Inventing.cs**: PassOn(teacher, learner, strength, shown) with Learning progress (+0.15 talk / +0.35 shown, chance 0.25×…, TalkCeiling 0.4); BestCase ×3; heard = 1 + 5p; Discover removes Learning; materials from sightings; "winter" and "dark" pressures from temperature and day length.
   - **Wayfinding.cs**: tracks noticed via TrailsNear within 300 m; gossip blurred about 700 m; ChooseCampSite(leader, min, max) from mental maps; TellPlaces sets KnowsFishing.

   **Activities**
   - **Activities/Decider.cs**:

   - **Activities/HearthActivities.cs**: DailyBurn(temperatureC) = 0.8 + max(0, 20 − T)×0.07; wood fallback searches the nearest visible forest.

4. **Errors and fixes**

   **Tooling and process**
   - **Analysis tables lost to the 2-hour background limit:** fixed by printing incrementally and using Start-Process for long runs.
   - **Builds failing because running exes locked DLLs** (v3 ran old code for 25 minutes): fixed by building to a separate `-o bin_vN` per experiment.
   - **dotnet not found:** use "/c/Program Files/dotnet/dotnet".
   - **WebLoader.Find failing from the scratchpad:** fixed by passing the path explicitly.
   - **A sed mangled backslashes in a path:** fixed with the Edit tool.
   - **Bash heredoc quoting failures:** fixed by writing Python patch files to the scratchpad.
   - **A `&&` chain stopped before the patch ran:** fixed by running the patch separately.
   - **Build errors:** Season ambiguity (used Core.Season); missing usings (Person, Interest); static Grown (used camp.Grown).

   **Ecology tuning**
   - **Snow too deep:** added settling.
   - **"Away" was a free safe haven for herds:** away herds now lose condition and die too.
   - **Wolves extinct, then over-effective:** fixed with week-scale hunger, compensatory Culled, scent range, encounter-based prey choice, and multi-day carcass feeding.
   - **Deer births suppressed:** fixed by setting pregnancy at the rut.

   **Hunting overkill**
   - Fixed with: room/huntRoom, canEat subtracting camp meat, the AnimalShare meat craving, real search, Shy, and tracking.

   **Famine**
   - Microbands from hard-times splits; demand sharing (SpareFor); bark too generous, then depleting and desperation-only.

   **Splitting**
   - The tiny-band ↔ join churn loop: hardship-only pull, Overwork threshold raised to 7 h, and a one-year cooldown.

   **Peoples and camps**
   - **Lone-person camp:** I restricted strike-outs to two or more adults. The user objected ("lone wolf people not allowed?"), so I restored lone strike-outs and added the loneliness pull plus own-tribe camps always known.
   - **Dogs vanishing when camps dissolved:** TakeDogs.
   - **Dog overbreeding:** breeding only above 0.4 fed.

   **Calibration**
   - Child mortality knobs double-counted with hunger: reverted to 0.003 and 4.

   **Violence**
   - **Inter-camp war:** camps of one people were treated as Outsiders. Fixed with SamePeople, CampRelation, shared culture and acquaintance.
   - **Fights too lethal:** reduced wounds and discounted grievance by the target's injury.

   **User feedback that changed the work**
   - Foresight was hard-coded (calendar window, hunting bonus, autumn 60 days). The user questioned it; I reworked it to learned seasonal leanness with a uniform keepable-food pull.
   - Bark: the user disliked it → removed → the user said it's OK as desperation-only → restored at low yield and depleting.
   - Off-map herd: the user said "the off map herd shouldnt exist".
   - Lone wanderers: the user wants them allowed.
   - Civil wars should be possible and cultures should vary; I made raids depend on CampRelation.

5. **Problem Solving**

   **Solved**
   - Off-map subsidy and runaway growth.
   - Deer wipeouts: deer now persist in nearly all worlds.
   - Microbands and churn reduced.
   - Discovery spread is now uneven.
   - Breakups now happen (15–40% of mothers have children by more than one father).
   - Dogs work when granted.
   - Tech → bigger groups confirmed: tech starvation 1–5% vs 14–59%; populations 1.5–2×, but spread over 7–10 camps.
   - Speed: hunting and the ecology are about 3–5× faster in those parts.
   - Knowledge leaks fixed; calendar rules replaced by conditions.

   **Ongoing**
   - Famine still 30–60% of recorded deaths (attribution question).
   - v27: every world shrinking, births about 56 per world in 20 years vs about 79 in v22.
   - Pairing: young women 0–25% partnered while men 85–100% in some worlds.
   - Violence: v27 average about 17 violent deaths per world per 20 years (range 0–58), still far above real rates. One runner world had 36 killings in 15 years, mostly in-camp food quarrels in hungry springs.
   - Single-camp start is demographically non-viable (pairing). kinCamps 2 is viable. This is the user's decision.

Ready to compact. I've saved the queue and where things stand in two places: the memory file (`anthro-overnight-queue`) and a "state at compaction" section at the end of `docs/PROGRESS.md`.

**Done since your last check-in:**

- technology → bigger groups confirmed (with a catch: the people spreads into 7–10 camps);
- breakups;

**Running now:** v28, 12 worlds over 60 years. It tests less lethal fights, which should cut the far-too-high killing rate (36 killings in 15 years in one world of about 70). It also tests that raiding a camp of your own people now depends on how you feel about them.

**Next, in order:**

5. Full test run (13 tests) and documentation.

**One decision for you:** a single starting camp isn't viable, because there aren't enough unpaired adults to form couples. Starting as one people in three related camps works. Which do you want as the default?

### Claude (Assistant)

Two things: what v28 showed, then marriage.

## v28 (stopped partway through compaction, but the first 20 years are in for all 12 worlds)

| | v27 | v28 |
|---|---|---|
| Violent deaths per world, 20 years | avg 17 (range 0–58) | **7 worlds 0–8, 4 worlds 18–52** |
| Births per world | ~56 | ~66 |
| Births vs deaths | every world shrinking | still every world shrinking (deaths ~95) |
| Share of deaths from starvation | 30–60% | still ~25–70% |

Violence now varies a lot between worlds. Most are peaceful and a few are violent, which matches what you asked for. The violent ones are still too violent, though. Starvation is still too high, and it's now the clear biggest problem, so it's next on the queue.

## Should marriage be hard-coded?

**No.** Here's how I'd draw the line: **hard-code the biology, let the institution be learned.**

**What humans actually do**
- **Polygyny** (one man, several wives) is allowed in about 85% of recorded societies. Even there, most marriages are monogamous, because few men can support two families. Among foragers, maybe 5–20% of men have a second wife. It gets much more common once wealth can be stored or inherited: herds, land, granaries.
- **Polyandry** (one woman, several husbands) is rare, around 1%. It shows up where land is scarce, as in Tibet, where brothers share one wife so the family farm isn't split.
- **"Partible paternity"** is a belief in some Amazonian groups that a child can have several fathers. Each man who slept with the mother helps provide for the child, and those children survive better.
- **Kids raised collectively** is actually the human baseline. Grandmothers, aunts, older siblings and other adults do a large share of the childcare, so that's not a stretch at all. The Mosuo in China go further: there is no marriage, and children are raised by the mother's family while fathers only visit.
- **"Only the king mates"** never happens biologically in humans. But extreme versions of it do happen through power: harems and despotic chiefs, with a handful of men fathering a huge share of children. That's something power produces, not a mating system.
- **Queen-only breeding** (like ants or bees) is outside humanity. It would take a different species over millions of years.

**What I'd build**
1. **Hard-code only the biology:**
   - people form attachment bonds;
   - jealousy exists;
   - men can't be sure a child is theirs;
   - pregnancy and nursing cost the mother;
   - children need years of provisioning.
2. **Make the rest learned customs, using the same expectation system** that already drives sharing and violence:
   - Is taking a second partner approved, tolerated, or punished?
   - Do children belong to the mother's family or the father's?
   - Does the whole camp feed children?
3. **Let the pressures choose the outcome:**
   - A woman (or her family) chooses between being a good provider's second wife and a poor provider's only wife. Where providers are very unequal, polygyny emerges. Where everyone is roughly equal, it stays rare.
   - When storage and inheritance appear, inequality rises and so does polygyny.
   - Where land is scarce, brothers sharing one wife can emerge.
   - Despotic polygyny comes from power, through the existing violence and status systems.
4. **Evolution paths (optional, slow):** if bonding strength and jealousy were heritable traits, mating psychology could drift over thousands of years. That's real evolution, but it would be invisible on a timescale of a few centuries. Nothing outside the human range would be possible.

This also fits the god angle: a god nudging a people toward or away from these customs creates real drama, like a jealous husband, a chief who hoards wives, or a camp that raises its orphans together.

**The question for you:** should mating psychology be heritable (an evolution path), or fixed biology with only the customs varying? I'd go with customs only for v1 and add heritable traits later.

Meanwhile I'm starting on starvation: first how hunger-weakened deaths get labelled, then gentler brakes before people starve.

### Claude (Assistant)

You're right, I skipped most of it. Taking each point:

**1. Customs only for marriage in v1.** Agreed. Marriage and child-raising will be learned customs, and the biology stays fixed:
- people form bonds and feel jealousy;
- a man can't be sure a child is his;
- pregnancy and nursing are costly;
- children need years of feeding.

I read the marriage code while the run was going, and it's a smaller job than I expected. "Partner" appears in only 22 places, and the camps already have two customs that form from what people actually do: who moves when a couple marries, and which parent keeps the children after a split.

For plural marriage I'd reuse the system that already makes some cultures violent and others peaceful. Taking a second spouse becomes a deed, like a theft or an attack. People see how it turns out (jealous fights, more food, children surviving or dying), remember it, and gossip about it. Each camp ends up with its own view of it, and nothing about it is fixed in code. The provider gap the breakup code already tracks becomes one of the pressures: where some men or women bring home far more than others, being someone's second spouse starts to look good.

**2. Accelerated evolution is already planned.** Right, that changes my point 4. Heritable mating psychology isn't a far-off extra. It's a natural later hook for the evolution system you're already building. I'll keep the marriage code shaped so that traits like bond strength and jealousy could become inherited later without rewriting it.

**3. Doesn't need to match foragers exactly.** Agreed, and I saved it to memory as a standing rule. Here's what it changes:
- **Violence:** I'll stop trying to push it down. v28 has most worlds peaceful and a few violent, and that's good variety. I'll only come back to it if it breaks the game.
- **Starvation:** I'm not chasing a target number. But one thing still conflicts with your balance goal: in v27 and v28 every world shrank, so humans don't reliably persist without a god. That's why I'm still looking at it, to understand it first and then fix only what's unnatural. If spring famines turn out to come from a real seasonal gap that people handle sensibly, I'd leave the harshness and treat it as drama for the god to step into.

**4. Is anything running?** Yes, as of a moment ago. After compaction nothing was running; v28 had been killed. Since then:
- I analysed v28's output.
- I ran one death-diagnostic world, which found that starvation deaths are almost all in a few bad springs.
- I just started a second run that logs each camp week by week through late winter and spring: snow, stored food, what digging for roots returns, and how people spend their hours.

When that finishes, I'll tell you what's causing the spring famines before I change anything. After that I'll build the marriage customs.

### Claude (Assistant)

The trace explains it. Here's what goes wrong in a bad year, step by step:

**1. Winter: people live entirely off stores, and nobody works.**
- From mid-winter on, the land gives almost nothing. In one camp, people gathered 6–22 rations a week against a need of 150–200.
- Even so, adults sleep about 11.5 hours, socialise 3–4 hours, and hunt about **0 hours**.
- In reality winter was prime hunting time: animals are weak, tracks show in the snow, and meat freezes so it keeps.

There are two reasons nobody goes out:
- **A trip needs 6 hours of daylight left.** On an 8-hour winter day, that leaves only the first two hours after sunrise to set out. That 6 is a number I hand-set, not something that follows from the trip.
- **The urge to store food only pulls toward hunting for people who know how to dry meat.** But in winter, meat freezes and keeps without any knowledge.

**2. People don't notice stores running out until it's too late.**
- Hunger only kicks in when a hearth has less than about 6 days of food left. That's also hand-set.
- So for weeks they watch the pile shrink and eat full rations. Then, with about 6 days left and the snow still deep, they finally start working, and it's too late.
- Nobody rations. Real people would have gone on short rations, everyone getting thinner, instead of feasting and then starving.

**3. Early spring: the snow is still on the ground.**
- Digging for roots returns about 10% of normal until the snow melts (roughly 3 weeks into spring).
- Even after the melt, food stays below need for another month or so.
- This part is real, and worth keeping as the harsh environment.

**4. During the famine, people still socialise 3–4 hours a day.** Hunger only partly cuts that.

Meanwhile, a neighbouring camp of the same people sits on 500+ rations with 9 people. That part is fine: camps don't pool food, and it's a nice spot for a god to step in.

**Proposed fixes (all behaviour, no new hard-coded numbers):**
- **(a) Judge stores against how long the lean stretch is expected to last,** instead of a fixed 6 days. People already learn how lean each season is, so they'd see the shortfall coming weeks ahead.
- **(b) Rationing:** when stores look short for the stretch ahead, hearths eat less per day. Hunger spreads as thinness rather than sudden death. Thin women conceive less, which is exactly the gradual brake you picked (option b).
- **(c) Winter hunting:** the store-up pull applies to hunting whenever meat will keep, so freezing weather counts, not only knowing how to dry it.
- **(d) Daylight:** size the trip to the light left (a short root dig near camp needs about 3 hours), instead of the fixed 6.

I expect this to cut the spring die-offs a lot without removing them, since a truly long winter will still kill. Want me to go ahead with (a)–(d)? While you decide, I'm starting the marriage customs.

Here's where things stand:

**Starvation:** I found the cause; details are in my message above. Bad springs follow winters where nobody hunts. People also ignore their shrinking stores until about 6 days of food are left, and never ration. I'm waiting on your OK for fixes (a)–(d).

**Plural marriage:** built, compiles cleanly, and running now on 10 worlds × 40 years. The test suite is running alongside.

What I built:
- **A person can have several spouses.** A married person who grows close to an unmarried one may take them as another spouse. It happens only if:
  - the newcomer prefers them to everyone free, knowing they'd be sharing the spouse;
  - the married person still wants it after weighing the hurt to their current spouses;
  - both sides' learned view of such marriages allows it.
- **It works the same for both sexes,** so one wife with several husbands is possible too. A woman with several husbands conceives by one of them, more likely the one she's closest to.
- **Jealousy is innate.** It's softened by a culture that approves and by fondness for the newcomer, so sisters sharing a husband (common in real polygyny) comes naturally. A hurt spouse may leave.
- **Providing now matters in who people want to marry,** for first marriages too. Since it's measured against others of the same sex, gender roles still aren't hard-coded.
- **The custom is learned.** Once a year, everyone looks at the plural households around them: are they eating better than the camp, are their children surviving, is there bad blood among the spouses? Members learn the most, onlookers a little, and the view spreads by talk like any other belief.

**Hand-set numbers, disclosed:**
- **Starting view:** each people starts with a random view of plural marriage, centred on neutral with a spread of 0.3.
- **Desire weights:**
  - a good provider counts 0.2 in desire;
  - sharing a spouse costs the newcomer 0.15 per existing spouse;
  - a marriage needs at least 0.3 fondness both ways;
  - the married person's pull must reach 0.5.
- **Learning rates:** 0.3 for people in the marriage, 0.03–0.08 for onlookers.

I'll report what emerges when the run finishes: which peoples take it up, how many hearths, and whether views diverge between peoples.

### Claude (Assistant)

**Tests:** all 13 pass. The 10-world, 40-year marriage run has also finished. Results are at the end.

**Your point on 4: yes, this is a gap.** Right now camps can't share food with each other, and there's no way to ask:
- Visitors get fed automatically by their hosts, with no choice involved.
- A guest who gets hungry goes home.
- Nobody ever walks to another camp because they're hungry.

So a starving camp next to a full one does nothing, which is unrealistic. Among real foragers, visiting relatives in other camps is the main famine insurance.

I'd build it the way you describe, reusing the machinery that already makes cultures vary:
1. **Hunger becomes a reason to visit.** It pulls people toward camps where they have kin and friends and that they believe are doing better.
2. **Hosts choose.** They decide whether to give, and whether to send food home with the guest, using the same give-or-refuse judgement as inside a camp. That judgement depends on what each person has learned giving to kin, friends, strangers or other peoples brings. So some cultures share freely across camps, some only with their own people, a few even with outsiders, and stingy ones refuse.
3. **Refusal has consequences.** A refusal is a deed that's remembered and gossiped about back home. It builds grievance against that camp, and grievance is what drives raids, so you get exactly the "won't share → raid" path you described. A hungry guest could also just steal.
4. Whole hungry families can already move to a better-off camp. That stays.

I'll add it to the starvation work, since it's the same problem. Starting on (a)–(d) plus this now.

**What plural marriage did across 10 worlds × 40 years:**
- It happened in every world: 11–50 plural marriages taken per run. At any moment, roughly 5–20% of married adults were in one.
- **Every people learned to dislike it.** Views started near neutral and all drifted to between −0.3 and −0.85, because jealousy and strife outweighed the benefits in practice. That's a believable outcome for small, fairly equal forager bands. The one I'd question: peoples ended up only mildly varied, with no people where it became the accepted norm.
- **One unrealistic result:** wives with several husbands were about as common as husbands with several wives. In real societies that ratio is roughly 1 to 100. The cause is that I left out two real biological asymmetries:
  1. **Children:** a man with two wives can father more children, but a woman with two husbands can't bear more. My learning step scores children as the share who survive, not the number born, so it misses this.
  2. **Paternity:** a husband who shares a wife can't know whether a child is his. That's the root of the stronger male sexual jealousy found across cultures.

Both count as biology rather than gender roles, so they fit the "hard-code biology, learn the customs" line you agreed to. I'd add them, which should make polygyny the common plural form and polyandry rare, and possibly give some peoples a reason to approve of it. Say the word and I'll add them after the starvation fixes.

### Claude (Assistant)

**Starvation fixes (a)–(d):** all four are built.
- (a) People judge their stores against how long they've learned the lean stretch lasts, including the rest of the current season. Worry about a shrinking store pulls them out to find food.
- (b) Hearths ration, but only while the land isn't keeping up: they spread what's left over the expected lean stretch, down to half portions at most. The automatic 150% feasting now only happens when the land is supplying enough, or when stores cover the whole lean stretch with room to spare.
- (c) Hunting and fishing to stock up now count whenever meat will freeze, not only for people who know how to dry it.
- (d) Gathering and fishing trips start with 3 hours of daylight left and hunts with 4, down from a fixed 6. The work stops in time to walk home before dark.

**Early v31 numbers (still running):** springs are much better fed, mostly 85–100% against 63–76% before. The two worlds still running at year 18 are larger than before and growing. Three finished worlds shrank anyway, mainly through violence and splitting into many small camps, with fewer births. I'll judge on all 10 worlds together.

**Cross-camp food sharing:** built, and running an 8-year test.
- When a hearth is running short and the land isn't keeping up, someone walks to another camp they know, within 25 km. They choose the camp by their ties there and by what asking those people has brought them before.
- A parent of small children can go if another adult stays home.
- Each host hearth decides with the same give-or-refuse judgement used inside a camp. That judgement depends on the culture's learned view of giving to kin, friends, strangers or outsiders.
- Gifts are carried home, up to what one person can carry, at most a week of food.
- A refusal goes into the asker's memory and spreads by gossip as grievance against that whole camp, which can lead to a raid. A gift lowers that grievance.

**Hand-set numbers in this change:** the 25 km asking range, a one-week carry limit, at most one food trip a week per person, the 3 and 4 hours of daylight, and half portions as the rationing floor.

The plural-marriage biology (more children for men with more wives; paternity uncertainty) is still waiting on your OK.

### Claude (Assistant)

## Feasting and rationing: learned or hard-coded?

**Feasting was a hard-coded rule I wrote earlier:** "with more than 3 days of food at home, eat 150% of need." Nothing was learned. The rule is right for fresh meat after a big kill, and real foragers did gorge then: the meat would rot otherwise, and eating it turns it into body fat, which is the best storage there is. It's wrong for winter nuts, which keep for months. So the rule itself was broken, not the world.

**How it interacts with spoilage:** the rule ignored spoilage, so stores that would have lasted got eaten early. I've now tied it to spoilage. People eat past need only when:
- they have more fresh meat and greens than the family can eat in about 2 days, before it rots; or
- they have keeping food beyond what the lean stretch calls for.

That follows from how fast each food spoils, which is already a fixed fact in the game. It isn't a culture rule.

**Rationing is a fixed decision rule fed by learned knowledge**, which is the same pattern as the rest of the game:
- **Fixed:** "while the land isn't keeping up, spread what's in store over the time you expect it to have to last." It's simple arithmetic, like a hunter choosing which deer to stalk.
- **Learned:** how long and how lean the hard stretch is, from people's own memories of getting thin in each season, which spread by talk. A people that has never had a bad spring won't ration, and gets caught out once. Survivors learn, and the lesson passes to their children and neighbours.
- **Hand-set:** the floor of half portions.

If you want it more cultural, rationing strictness could become a learned custom: people who feasted and then starved learn to ration harder, and people who rationed and then watched food rot learn to loosen up. I'd hold that for now unless you want it.

## How v31 went (10 worlds × 40 years)

The famine fixes did less than I hoped, and they changed what starvation looks like:

| | v30 (before) | v31 (with fixes) |
|---|---|---|
| Starvation deaths, years 1–20 (all worlds) | 286 | 277 |
| Final populations | 36–80 (total 587) | 10–157 (total 692) |
| Births / deaths | 1324 / 1464 | 1468 / 1503 |

- **Early on, springs are better fed,** but starvation hardly fell. Populations grew instead.
- **Outcomes split.** Four worlds boomed to 90–157 people. In those, starvation in years 21–40 is the population hitting what the land can carry, with bad springs trimming it back. That matches what you said earlier was the right thing: growth that stops at carrying capacity.
- **Four worlds collapsed** to 10–35 people, mostly through violence and splitting into many small camps, not famine.

**A dog problem:** one world reached **231 dogs for 157 people.** I already limited breeding to well-fed dogs, but they still multiply when times are good, and they eat scraps people could use. I'll check whether they're adding to famine.

## The two plural-marriage changes are in (running as v33)

1. **Children are counted by whose they are.** Each person in a plural marriage learns from their own children in it. A man with two wives has more children; a woman with two husbands doesn't, and each of her husbands has fewer. Nothing tells the game that polygyny pays and polyandry doesn't; it follows from who bears the children.
   - Onlookers judge by people like themselves. Men watch how the husband does, women watch how the wives do. Men and women can now come to see the custom differently.
2. **Jealousy scales with what's at stake for the body:**
   - **A man can't be sure a child is his,** so sharing a wife puts his offspring at stake.
   - **A woman's children are certainly hers,** so what she risks is her husband's food and care, which counts more the better he provides.
   - It also scales with the share of the spouse each person loses. Going from one spouse to two hurts more than going from two to three.

v33 also includes cross-camp food sharing and the spoilage-based feasting rule. It's running now: 10 worlds × 40 years.

| World | Pop Y1 → peak → Y40 | Camps at end | Violent deaths (yr 1–10) | Starvation (40 yr) | Stages known Y40 | Outcome |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 9 | 71 → 164 → 157 | 9 | 0 | 47 | 20 | boom, now at capacity |
| 5 | 68 → 147 → 147 | 8 (13 at peak) | 0 | 70 | 14 | boom |
| 1 | 65 → 114 → 91 | 4 | 3 | 93 | 17 | grew, then capped |
| 2 | 69 → 105 → 73 | 3 | 0 | 91 | 16 | grew, then a bad decade |
| 3 | 69 → 87 → 81 | 4 | 0 | 67 | 10 | stable |
| 4 | 74 → 94 → 28 | 2 | 9 | 98 | 15 | **slow famine spiral** |
| 7 | 72 → 82 → 10 | 2 | 0 | 86 | 9 | **famine crash, near extinct** |
| 6 | 58 → 58 → 41 | 2 | **28** | 19 | 8 | **violence crash**, then recovery |
| 8 | 72 → 72 → 29 | 3 | **34** | 35 | 7 | **violence crash** |
| 10 | 47 → 57 → 35 | 4 | **46** | 9 | 9 | **violence crash** |

All 10 peoples survived 40 years. Four grew, one held steady, and five shrank.

## The collapses: two different ways down

**1. Violent cultures (worlds 6, 8, 10)**
- These lost 28–46 people to killing in the first decade, out of 50–70. That's **5–7% of the population killed per year.** The most violent real forager societies on record ran around 0.5–1% per year.
- The killings are mostly inside a camp: "killed by Torish", "killed by Dal", repeat killers.
- Births also collapse in those years (21–28 per decade against 45–64 in calm worlds), because the dead are young adults and feuds break up families.
- Once the population is small, the killing stops and they stabilise around 30–40.

You said violence varying between worlds is fine, and the variation itself is good. But at this level a violent culture tears itself apart within 5 years, which breaks "humans persist without a god." I'd tone down the extreme end without removing it, so violent peoples bleed rather than self-destruct.

**2. Famine spirals (worlds 4, 7)**
- **World 7:** five bad springs in the first decade killed 65 people (72 → 30). With so few left, births fell to about 1 a year. Then one more bad decade left 10 people.
- **World 4:** famine every decade (29, 21, 32, 16 deaths) despite knowing a lot, including cold storage and hearths. Births fell decade by decade, from 45 to 10. Starving women stop conceiving, which is the brake you picked, but it compounds into a death spiral.

Neither world was short of food over the year: they produced 1.15–1.35 times what they needed. It all comes down to the spring gap.

## Carrying capacity: are we where we want to be?

- **Density:** the booming worlds level off around 150–165 people on the 1,600 km² map, about **0.1 people per km²**. Real temperate foragers ran about 0.05–0.3 per km², so this is plausible, toward the sparse end.
- **What limits them is right:** not total food, but the worst season. At the cap they still produce about 1.25 times their yearly need and throw a lot away. **Spoiled food equals 40–98% of what gets eaten**, mostly summer and autumn gluts that nobody preserves. Real seasonal foragers lived exactly this way, bottlenecked by late winter and spring, and storage was what raised their numbers.
- **This sets up the tech lever well:** better storage → surviving the spring gap → a higher ceiling. That's the "tech lets bands get bigger" path you wanted, and it shows. The two worlds that know the most (9 and 5) boomed, and the three that know the least (8, 10, 7) collapsed.
- **What's off:** world 4 learned a lot and still spiralled, so knowing isn't enough unless people actually use it at scale. That's worth a look.

## Splits and camps (the player-management concern)

- **Splitting:** 12–34 splits per world over 40 years, so up to about one a year in growing worlds. People also move between camps constantly: 25 to 270 individual moves over 40 years.
- **Camps:**
  - 2–4 in small worlds;
  - **8–13 in booming ones**, many tiny (final sizes like 47/32/28/18/14/5/2/1).
- **Peoples:** always 1. No schism ever happened in 40 years.

The fluid camp membership is realistic; forager bands constantly reshuffle. But your v1 target was 1–2 camps you really play with and 3–5 at most. Booming worlds still blow well past that. This is still open, and I'd raise it as a design choice: let camps of one people merge back more readily, or treat small satellite camps as part of the main one for the player.

## Discoveries

- **Pace:** 7–20 stages known by year 40. Without a god, that might be **too fast** for "advance slowly." These stages (spear-thrower, cordage, hearths, cold storage) took real humans thousands of years each. Worth deciding what pace you want; the discovery rate is still your call.
- **Impact:** strong. It's the clearest predictor of which worlds thrive.
- **Knowledge gets lost:** in world 9, storytelling went from 84% to 12% of people as knowers died and camps split. Torch-making fell from 100% to 73% in world 6. That's realistic, and good drama for a god to step into.
- **Dogs:** world 9 reached the "wolves lurk near camp" stage (67% knew it), but nobody went on to taming pups, so there are **0 dogs anywhere**. That's the next stage, and it's by design. My earlier "231 dogs" was a misread column; that was people moving between camps.

## Ecology and animals

Very stable and healthy:
- **Deer:** 4,700 → 5,500–6,000.
- **Boar:** about 2,000 → 2,700.
- **Rabbits:** 8,200 → 14,000, filling up to capacity from the 80% start.
- **Wolves:** steady at 25–38.
- **Mammoths:** migratory, so they're off the map when counted. They still kill 4–9 people per world when they pass through.

Human hunting takes only 2–6% of the deer a year, so people barely dent the herds. That's realistic at 0.1 people per km². **One exception:** in world 5, people learned to hunt boar heavily in the last years (1,236 boar in year 40) and the boar population fell from 2,850 to 1,550. That's the first real human-driven decline, and it emerged entirely on its own. Something to watch.

## Seasons and climate

- **Spring is the one real bottleneck:** 0–6 bad springs per decade depending on the world. Bad winters are rare now that people store and ration.
- Weather varies by year and by place across the map; some worlds just get luckier runs.

## Behaviour

- **Work:** 3.9–5.4 hours of food-getting per adult per day, in line with real foragers (the Hadza are around 4–6).
- **Leisure:** 3–4.4 hours of socialising, 1–2.7 of rest. A lot, but foragers do socialise a lot.
- **Diet:** meat is 12–67% of food depending on the world. That's the real forager range, and it varies by culture and land.
- **Lifespan:**
  - Life expectancy at birth: 30–39 in good worlds, which is spot on for foragers. Only 8–14 in collapsing ones.
  - Most deaths are infants dying of lung sickness and starvation. Respiratory infection really is the top forager killer.
- **Fertility is a bit high:** 5–8.5 children per woman, against a real 4–6.5. It's offset by high infant mortality.
- **Pairing:** mostly fine. Small worlds show odd gaps, like no women aged 25–34 partnered, but those are a handful of people.
- **Plural marriage (before the biology change):**
  - 9–25 plural hearths per 100 married adults in big worlds.
  - Every people disapproved of it (views −0.24 to −0.77).
  - Wives with several husbands were as common as husbands with several wives. v33 should fix that.

## Against our goals

| Goal | Status |
|---|---|
| Humans persist without a god | Mostly: 10/10 survived 40 years, but 2–3 came close to dying out |
| A single small band dying out is realistic | Yes, world 7 got down to 10 people |
| Advance slowly without a god | **Probably too fast:** 7–20 stages in 40 years |
| Tech → bigger, healthier groups | **Yes, strongly** |
| Cultures vary (violent/peaceful, customs) | Yes, but violent ones are **too** violent |
| Player manages 1–2 camps, 3–5 max | **No:** booming worlds spread to 8–13 camps |
| Ecology realistic and stable | Yes |

## What I'd do next, in order

1. **Wait for v33** (cross-camp sharing and the marriage biology), which may soften the famine spirals.
2. **Tone down the extreme end of violence,** so violent cultures bleed rather than self-destruct.
3. **The spoilage-versus-spring mismatch:** see why big summer gluts don't get preserved even where people know drying.
4. **Two decisions for you:** discovery pace, and the camp-count cap or merging for the player.

Want me to start on 2 and 3 while v33 runs?

### Claude (Assistant)

I've traced what drives each of these. Taking your questions in turn.

## 1. "A fixed decision rule fed by learned knowledge": what's written vs learned

Every choice a person makes works the same way. Each possible action gets a **score from a formula I wrote**, and they do the highest-scoring one. What makes people and cultures differ is the **inputs** to those formulas:
- **Inherited traits** (temper, boldness, warmth, strength…), which vary person to person and are passed to children.
- **Learned beliefs**: how well hunting has paid lately, what stealing from kin leads to, how lean spring gets. They come from each person's own experience and spread by talk.
- **Hand-set weights** inside the formulas (for example, hunger counts 1.5×).

How that splits for the main behaviours:

| Behaviour | Written (formula / rule) | Learned or inherited | Hand-set numbers |
|---|---|---|---|
| Which food work (forage / hunt / fish / trap) | score = need × learned food-per-hour × interest | food per hour of each activity; where things are (mental map, gossip); which hunting tactic works on which animal; how dangerous each animal is | need weights; reference yield 0.4/h |
| Hunting a particular animal | follow the freshest trail or sighting; best expected value | sightings and tracks; tactics; caution | track lifetimes; search ≤ 8 h |
| Storing for lean times | shortfall = stores vs expected lean stretch | how lean each season gets, from having been thin | — |
| Rationing / feasting | spread stores over the lean stretch; eat extra only what would rot | lean seasons | floor of half portions; "2 days" of fresh food |
| Asking, giving, refusing, stealing, fighting | score = learned outcome for that relation + guilt (innate) + hunger + anger | what each act has brought them, per relation (kin/friend/stranger/outsider); seeded differently per people | culture seed ranges; wound sizes |
| Gang food thefts, raids | same, plus who joins | same | — |
| Marriage | mutual "best choice" pairing; innate jealousy | attraction tastes per camp; plural-marriage view; who moves at marriage; who keeps the children | fondness thresholds |
| Moving camp | proposal + weighted vote | remembered value of places | moving costs |
| Splitting | discontent = hunger + crowding + overwork + feuds | — (mostly state, not learned) | **7 h overwork; crowding scale 60** |
| Inventing | chance from need and materials | spread by teaching and watching | **discovery rate** |

**Is this the right approach?** It's the standard way to build agents like these, and it's what keeps the game explainable: you can always ask why someone did something. The alternative, behaviour learned from scratch by trial and error, would be slow, unpredictable and opaque.

**Where your worry is valid:**
1. **Weights that encode a cultural choice should be learned instead.** I've been converting these one by one (who moves at marriage, plural marriage, how lean seasons are). The remaining risky ones are the split thresholds and the rationing and sharing policies. These could become culture-level habits that drift with experience, like the existing customs.
2. **Some rules were simply wrong**, like feasting on winter nuts. The test I use: does the rule describe how a body or the physical world works (fixed is fine), or what a culture chooses to do (should be learned)?

I'd like to do a proper pass over every decision formula, marking each one physics, biology, decision-maker (fixed is fine) or culture (should be learned), and converting the culture ones. It's the same idea as the hard-coding audit, aimed at behaviour.

## 2. The "repeat killers": a camp-wide brawl, and partly a bug in my tool

The repeat killers weren't serial murderers. I traced world 8's violent year:
- A big camp of 40 split off at the end of a hungry spring. Within three weeks, that new camp had a string of **gang food thefts** ("Tasun and 3 others tried to take Nakash's food and were driven off"), dozens of them, mostly against the same few families.
- On one day, one theft attempt turned into a **brawl that killed 15 people.** The people listed as killing 2–3 each (Uln, Keral, Sum…) were all fighters in that single brawl.

Hungry people banding together to take food is logical. The fact that it **kills 15 people in one go** isn't, and here's why it happens: a fight is a series of one-on-one clashes, and nobody ever backs off. Badly wounded people keep getting picked to fight again until they die. Real fights mostly end when one side is hurt and runs or gives in. My fix: badly hurt people yield or flee, the way a sensible person would. Violence can still be high, but it won't be massacre-by-pileup.

**The bug part:** those deaths never appeared in my reports because my analysis tool only started listening to a new camp's chronicle at the end of the year it was founded. That's a reporting bug, not a game bug; easy fix.

## 3. Discovery pace

Agreed, it's too fast. 7–20 stages in 40 years means a new invention every 2–5 years with no god. What should it be without a god? I'd suggest roughly **1–3 stages per 40 years**, with a god able to push that up 5–10×. Give me a number and I'll tune toward it, then check that the god can actually speed it up.

## 4. Camps: what is one, and did they grow with tech?

**A camp** is the group that lives at one spot: they sleep there, share hearths, see each other every day, gossip and share food. A **people** is the wider group of camps that know each other, marry across and share a culture.

**Tech did not make camps bigger.** I grouped every 5-year window from v31 and v33 by how many inventions were widely known:

| Inventions widely known (≥30% of people) | Largest camp | Average camp | Camps | Population |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 0–2 | 30 | 17 | 3.8 | 60 |
| 6–8 | 31 | 16 | 4.3 | 63 |
| 12–20 | 38 | 17 | 5.7 | 89 |

Tech gave **more people but in more camps of the same size.** The split pressures don't respond to tech enough:
- **Bad springs** still cause hunger, which drives splits.
- **Crowding** depends only on camp size and how many people you're close to, and nothing raises that ceiling.
- Nothing pulls people **together**, such as defence against raids or a rich spot worth staying at.

For your scattered → centralised progression, I'd add three things:
1. **Storage and rich, fixed resources** (fish runs, tended grain, nut groves) make staying put worth more. That's how real villages started.
2. **Social technology** (story, ritual, burial, shared names) lets people bond with more camp-mates, so a bigger camp doesn't feel crowded.
3. **Threat pulls people together.** Raids nearby make bigger camps safer, and so more attractive.

**Player experience:** something like "main camp 47, satellites 32/28/18/14, plus a few families of 1–5" could work well if the player sees **one people, with a main settlement and satellites around it**. The tiny families run themselves and only surface when something dramatic happens. As tech pulls the satellites into the centre, the map visibly turns into a town. That's your city-builder arc.

## 5. Spoilage vs drying

It is strange, and here's why it happens:
- **Drying takes almost no time:** 0.02–0.06 hours per adult per day, a minute or two.
- **The work rate is far too low:** 5 rations per 3-hour session (10 with racks). Real people can slice and hang several rations' worth of meat per hour, and the sun does the rest.
- **Only people who know drying can do it,** and in some worlds that's 0%.
- **The wish to dry only gets strong when people expect a lean season.** In summer, with lots of fresh meat, it barely registers.

So the waste is real and mostly comes from a hand-set rate that's too low, plus little sense of urgency. I'd fix the rate (~3× faster, closer to real), and let spoiled food push people toward drying. Seeing food rot is exactly the kind of experience that teaches it.

## 6. Why the population ceiling is low (0.1/km²)

At the cap, people still produce about 1.25× what they need over the year, so total food isn't the limit. The ceiling is low because:
1. **The spring gap:** stores run out and little gets dried (point 5).
2. **Almost no fishing:** fish is 1–10% of their food. Rivers and fish runs were what pushed real temperate foragers to high densities, sometimes over 1 per km². Fishing is limited by knowledge and gear, and the runs come on fixed calendar days (already on the hard-coding list).
3. **Starting tech is simple.** For people without storage or fishing gear, 0.05–0.1 per km² is actually realistic.

**The real problem:** **the ceiling barely rises with tech.** Fixing drying, fishing and the pull toward settling should let advanced peoples reach 0.3+ per km², which is also what makes centralisation possible.

## 7. Plural-marriage biology: didn't work yet, and why

The biology changes how people **learn about and react to** plural marriage, but not **whether they enter one**. That decision is driven mostly by fondness, and sharing costs the newcomer the same whether they're a man or a woman. The fix: the stakes apply at the moment of deciding.
- A man becoming a co-husband weighs his uncertain fatherhood.
- A woman becoming a co-wife weighs half of a good provider against all of a poor one.
- The one taking the extra spouse anticipates what their current spouses stand to lose.

## Proposed order

1. Fights end when someone's badly hurt, and fix my reporting tool. (Logic fixes.)
2. Plural marriage: stakes at the point of decision.
3. Drying: realistic rate, and rot teaching people to preserve.
4. Slow discoveries to your target. **Needs your number.**
5. The audit of decision rules (point 1), turning culture-like choices into learned customs.
6. Things that pull people together: storage and fixed rich resources, social tech, threat. Plus fishing and fish runs.

Want me to start 1–3 now while you think about the discovery pace?

### Claude (Assistant)

All 20 runs are going. Here's the fight design for you to react to before I build it.

## Fights: how they work now, and the problems

Right now a "fight" is **one exchange**:
- Whoever is stronger, angrier or luckier wins.
- The loser takes a random wound, and **dies once their accumulated injuries pass 95%**.
- Nobody chooses how far to go. A shove over food and a revenge killing are the same event.
- Emotions only decide **whether** someone attacks, not **how hard**.
- Group fights are a list of random one-on-ones, and **nobody backs off**. The wounded keep getting picked until they die, which is how one theft attempt killed 15.
- Culture affects only **whether** violence happens, through what each person has learned hitting brings. There's no culture of **how far** violence goes.

## Proposed design

**1. Intent: how far each fighter means to go.** Each person in a fight has an aim:
- **Display**: shouting, threatening, shoving.
- **Hurt**: a beating, to teach a lesson.
- **Kill.**

The aim comes from:
- **Emotion:** rage and hate toward that person (built-up grievances, revenge for kin), desperation (starving children), humiliation.
- **Restraint:** love, kinship, guilt, fear of the other's strength, fear of what the killing will bring (revenge, exile).
- **Culture:** what this people has learned about **how far is acceptable, toward whom.** This is the part that makes cultures differ:
  - one people where duels to the death are honourable;
  - one where drawing blood within the camp means exile;
  - one where outsiders can be killed freely, but kin never;
  - one that backs down from everything.

Each of these is learned from outcomes and spreads by talk like the other customs, with each people starting from its own random seed. No blanket rule.

**2. A fight is a sequence of exchanges, not one roll.** After each exchange, each side decides whether to keep going: resolve (rage, desperation, pride, what the culture says of backing down) against fear (own wounds, the other's strength). The losing side can **yield, flee or beg**. The winner then decides whether to stop or press on. Pressing on against someone who has yielded only happens with real intent to kill, and the culture judges that harshly or not at all.

**3. Weapons and the body.**
- Bare hands rarely kill.
- A spear or club in hand (hunters carry them) makes "hurt" far more dangerous.
- How badly someone is hurt depends on intent, weapon, strength and luck.
- Wounds can still turn fatal later through infection (already in the game).

**4. Group fights are side against side, not a list of duels.**
- Each person chooses whom to face: protect your kin, go for the one you hate, avoid the strongest.
- **Bystanders choose too:** join their kin's side, step in as a peacemaker, or stay out. Peacemaking is also a learned custom, and the most common real reason fights end.
- **Morale:** as a side takes losses, people start breaking away. When enough break, the whole side runs. Most deaths in real small-scale warfare come from **dawn raids and routs**, not drawn-out brawls, and this reproduces that naturally.

**5. Aftermath.** A killing sets off reactions that depend on the culture:
- revenge obligations for kin (already in the game);
- exile, or payment of compensation;
- or honour and standing for the killer, in cultures that admire it.

These outcomes feed back into each person's learned view of violence.

**Hand-set numbers, disclosed:** wound sizes per intent and weapon; the injury level at which people break and run (only a starting point, since it shifts with emotion and culture); the starting ranges of the cultural seeds.

This turns "violence level" into something with texture: why it happened, how far it went, who stepped in, and what it cost. That's also the material you need for story and drama.

## In progress now

- **2. Plural marriage:** the biological stakes now apply when people decide whether to enter one.
- **3. Drying:**
  - three times faster, a realistic slicing-and-hanging rate;
  - food rotting recently makes people keener to preserve.
- **4. Discoveries:** the base rate is now 6× slower, aiming for 1–3 new things per 40 years without a god. You can start a run with any set of technologies to test their effect; the game already supported that.
- **Reporting tool:** it now catches events in camps founded mid-year.

**Running now (20 worlds):**
- **A:** 10 worlds with the slower discoveries.
- **B:** 5 worlds that start with a settling toolkit: drying racks, caches and pits, fish spears and weirs, returning to and staying at one base camp, storytelling and ritual, huts, harvesting and clearing wild grain. This tests directly whether tech produces bigger, settled camps.
- **C:** 5 worlds that start with 2 unrelated peoples, with different cultures and no shared kin.

**Next, while those run:**
- **5:** the audit of decision rules.
- **6:** the "pull together" mechanics for settling: rich fixed resources worth staying for, social tech that lets bigger groups bond, safety in numbers.

Farming proper (domesticated crops and animals, villages) would come after 6, on top of the grain tending and base camps that already exist.

Want me to build the fight design as written, or change anything first?

### Claude (Assistant)

Morning. Everything we discussed is built and tested, with one caveat on settling below. All 15 tests pass, and nothing was committed to git. I checked the final version against the build from before last night on the same 20 fresh worlds, 40 years each:

| | Before | Now |
|---|---|---|
| Final population, all 20 worlds | 1,261 | 1,100 |
| Births | 2,989 | 2,861 |
| Starvation deaths | 1,395 | 1,570 |
| Violent deaths | 397 | 137 |
| Violent worlds | 7 of 20 | 2 of 20 |

So humans persist, survival is a bit harder, and violence is rarer but still varies a lot between peoples. That matches your balance target.

## What got built

**1. Fights (your design).** Each fighter means to show anger, hurt, or kill. That comes from what their people accept toward that kind of person (kin, friend, stranger, outsider), plus rage, love, guilt and fear.
- Fights are a few exchanges. People give way or run; onlookers join their side or step in as peacemakers; losing sides break and flee.
- Cultures differ in how far violence goes, in honour (backing down is shameful or not) and in peacemaking. Each killing shifts what the culture accepts.
- **Getting it believable took 8 rounds.** The flaws I found and fixed:
  - 15 people died in one pile-up.
  - Hungry people meant to kill over food.
  - A loop of 929 food thefts a year in one camp.
  - Families fought back against crowds instead of giving way.
  - Most killing blows hit people who were already badly hurt.
  - Anger was never spent after a fight.

  Now every killing is by someone who meant to kill. Violent peoples show realistic blood-feud chains.

**2. Plural marriage.** The biology now applies when people decide, not just afterwards: a co-husband risks his fatherhood, a co-wife shares provisioning. Polygyny is now 2–3 times as common as polyandry, as in real societies. Every people so far comes to disapprove of plural marriage, by varying amounts.

**3. Drying and discoveries.**
- Drying is 3× faster, and seeing food rot makes people keener to preserve.
- Discoveries are 6× slower: about 3 widely known after 40 years without a god, slightly above your 1–3 target.

**4. The decision audit** ([AUDIT-DECISIONS.md](Anthro/docs/AUDIT-DECISIONS.md)) sorts every decision rule into biology, plain decision logic, learned, or cultural choice baked in. Fixed: rationing strictness, when it's OK to ask for food, how much elders are listened to, and how prestigious hunting is are now learned customs, seeded differently for each people.

**5. Settled life.**
- Fish runs follow river warmth instead of calendar dates.
- Food too heavy to carry, built houses, tended grain and hand stencils all make leaving costlier. Each family weighs what it would lose.
- After a raid, small groups don't split off and bigger camps look safer.
- Feasts in times of plenty bind near-strangers, especially with ritual, rites and body paint.
- Grinding stones now matter for nuts and wild grain. Healing plants help the sick recover. Pit-fired pots and ochre got small effects.
- The new feature switches let me turn any of this off to test its effect.

**6. Tech review.** Every technology family was tested against the same worlds without it:
- **Large gains (+35 to +58 people):** spear-thrower, bow, fishing, bone harpoons, spears, shelter, traps.
- **Little or no food effect:** wayfinding, language, ochre and base camps.

That fits your point that tech doesn't always mean more density: those are about range and social scale, not food.

## The caveat on settling

With the starting toolkit, a people's camps drop from about 12 to 7, and the main camp holds half the people instead of a third. That's real progress, but not a town yet. The total population in those worlds also came out lower than before (128 against 208), which I haven't looked into yet.

## A mistake I made and caught

Mid-night results looked fine on 6–10 worlds, but a 20-world comparison showed I'd **halved population**. Testing each new feature in turn, again on 20 worlds, traced it to **demand sharing as I'd built it**:
- Adults spent 1.2 hours a day asking each other for food instead of producing it.
- Everyone got thinner, and births fell.

The fix: asking is driven only by real need, and the custom changes how hungry someone must be before asking, not who counts as short. The lesson I've recorded: never judge a change on fewer than 20 worlds, because random variation between worlds is larger than the effects I'm measuring.

## Reflection against the original plan ([REFLECTION.md](Anthro/docs/REFLECTION.md))

**Where we've stayed true:**
- People decide by need, what they expect, their traits and their culture, as the plan specified. That's what you were worried about, and it's the agreed design working.
- "Nothing scheduled, nothing global" is enforced, with calendar rules steadily replaced by conditions.
- The social layer was built as planned, from simple acts plus learning.
- Every death has a cause, and tragedies trace back to something visible.
- Conflict varies by culture.

**Where we've drifted:**
- **The god layer still doesn't exist.** It was the first milestone and the top priority in the 10-04 review. Every session since has deepened the simulation instead.
- Religion by misinterpretation hasn't been started.
- The discovery web grew from about 10 technology families to 27 (144 steps).
- The world is grounded in Earth data, with no plan yet for alien species.
- Evolution hasn't been built.
- Unity hasn't been started, even though the plan named art as the bottleneck.

## Recommended next steps

1. **The god layer, without graphics yet:** favor and belief, prayers as requests, omens and placing resources, nudging people at turning points, and religion forming as people misread what you do.
2. **A simple-shapes Unity slice,** since art is the bottleneck.
3. Keep simulation tuning to short sessions alongside: settling strength, discovery pace, how two peoples interact, and giving the elders and hunting customs a way to change with experience.

**Decisions I need from you:** whether you agree the god layer comes next, and whether to nudge discovery pace down a little more.

All results are in [PROGRESS.md](Anthro/docs/PROGRESS.md), and the saved queue in memory is updated.

### Claude (Assistant)

Not yet, but close. Noted as a standing rule: the god layer waits until the base sim passes this checklist. Here it is against everything we've set as goals:

| # | Requirement | Status |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Without a god, humans persist, but survival isn't easy | ✅ 19 of 20 worlds stay above 20 people at 40 years; bands can die out |
| 2 | Advance slowly without help | ⚠️ About 3 discoveries per 40 years, against your 1–3. A small adjustment |
| 3 | Tech → bigger, more settled camps (your city-builder arc) | ❌ **Biggest gap.** Camps drop from 12 to 7, but there's no real move toward a town yet. Tech-head-start worlds also ended with fewer people than before, and I haven't looked into why |
| 4 | Food and starvation are natural and understood | ✅ Explained down to specific springs and decisions |
| 5 | Ecology stable and believable | ✅ Watch one thing: people drove boar numbers down in one world |
| 6 | Cultures vary (violence, sharing, marriage, customs), for logical reasons | ✅ Within one people. ⚠️ **Two peoples meeting has barely been examined** (contact, war, absorption) |
| 7 | Nothing hard-coded that should be learned | ⚠️ Mostly done. Left: the thresholds that trigger splits, the elders' voice and hunting-prestige customs (seeded, but nothing changes them with experience), and some hand-set weights |
| 8 | Fits your player scale (1–2 camps you really play, 3–5 peoples) | ⚠️ Peoples never split in 40 years, so the multi-people side is untested |
| 9 | Stable over generations | ❌ **Untested.** Every run has been 40 years, but the game spans many generations. It needs 100–200-year runs for slow drift, collapse, runaway growth, and cultures ending up identical or extreme |
| 10 | Fast enough for real-time play | ❓ Not measured since the big additions |

**What's left before the god layer, in order:**
1. **Long runs:** 20 worlds × 150 years, to find what breaks slowly. Cheap to run, and most likely to surface something new.
2. **Settling progression.** This is real work: rich places worth staying at all year (fish weirs, nut groves, tended grain), and storage making winter safe enough that camps stop scattering. I'd also find out why the tech-head-start worlds shrank.
3. **Two peoples together:** contact, trade or raiding, absorption, and whether peoples ever split in long runs.
4. **Small cleanups:** discovery pace, giving the elders and hunting customs a way to change with experience, a speed check.

Agriculture proper (crops and villages) I'd leave for the next milestone, as originally planned. One note so it doesn't block forever: some targets ("the god improves the odds", "the god speeds discovery") can only be checked once the god exists. I'll mark those as "checked with the god" rather than holding everything up.

Rough size: items 1, 3 and 4 are a session or two together, and item 2 is the main effort.

Want me to start with the long runs, since they might reorder the rest?

### Claude (Assistant)

Going through your points:

**Discoveries:** 3 per 40 years it is.

**Violence: you're right, it's too rare as drama.** Here's the spread across the final 20 worlds:
- **7 worlds had no fights at all** in 40 years. Not one shove.
- **8 worlds had plenty of conflict but almost no deaths.** One had 1,465 fights and food thefts and zero killings.
- **3 worlds were lethal.** In one, *every* gang theft ended in a death, which is too extreme even for a violent culture.

So conflict, the part that makes drama, is missing from a third of worlds, and lethal violence is concentrated in a few. The cause: peoples start with a strong learned expectation that hitting anyone goes badly. Combined with tonight's restraint fixes, some peoples simply never fight.

What I'd aim for (your call): **conflict in nearly every world**, whether quarrels, thefts, jealousy or feuds, **mostly non-lethal, with killing common in some cultures and rare in others**. I'd get there through wider culture seeds and real conflict sources (rivalry over partners, theft, insult, leadership), not a quota. And I'd fix the culture where every fight kills.

**The 3-exchange cap: agreed, it's a hard-coded patch.** I'll replace it with what actually ends fights: exhaustion builds with every exchange, along with fear and wounds, and onlookers step in. Fights will end on their own, at different lengths.

**"Stable over generations", the reminder:** every run so far is 40 years, about one and a half generations, but the game spans many. Slow problems only show up over 100–200 years:
- populations slowly dying out or exploding;
- cultures drifting to extremes or all becoming the same (for example, every people already ends up disapproving of plural marriage);
- knowledge being lost faster than it's found;
- ecology slowly degrading;
- performance falling as populations grow.

It's a long-run check, not new features.

**Tech and settlement: good catch.** I'd base it on the archaeological record:

| Settlement form | Real-world example | What it took | People |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Mobile camps** | most foragers | basic tools | 15–50 |
| **Seasonal gathering at rich spots** | many foragers | a rich seasonal resource (fish run, nut harvest) plus ritual | 50–200, for weeks |
| **Semi-settled foraging villages** | Natufians, the Pacific Northwest, Jōmon Japan | **rich, reliable, storable** foods (salmon, acorns) plus storage plus houses, with **no farming** | 50–300 |
| **Towns and cities** | after farming | **agriculture** (and herding) | thousands |

So forager technology should allow at most **semi-settled villages at the richest places**, and only where those places exist. Cities need farming, which we've agreed belongs to the next milestone. Under that design, a map without salmon or acorns would *never* settle without farming. That's realistic, and an interesting puzzle for a god.

I'd fold this into the settlement work: classify each technology by what it does to settlement (pulls people together, lets them stay put, makes them more mobile, binds larger groups socially), then check against the measurements. Agriculture becomes the gate to towns later.

**Two peoples meeting:** light testing only, no economics until the god layer.

**Speed first: agreed, it speeds up everything else.** Starting now with profiling.

**Order:**
1. Speed.
2. Fight endings without the cap, and making conflict common.
3. Long runs.
4. Tech audit plus settlement forms.
5. A light two-peoples check, plus continuing the hard-coding audit throughout.

### Claude (Assistant)

Progress since your last messages:

**Done**
- **Speed:** about 18% faster. More importantly, it isn't a bottleneck: a 150-year world takes about 8 minutes, and real-time play has about 15× headroom.
- **Fights end on their own:** exhaustion, wounds and fear, yielding, routs or peacemakers end them. No more 3-exchange cap.
- **Conflict is cultural:**
  - Peoples differ in whether they take sides (brawls and feuds) or keep out, and whether duels are how grudges get settled.
  - There are ambushes of people alone outside camp; when the victim dies, the family blames someone, sometimes the wrong person.
  - Gang thefts only form where the culture closes ranks.
  - Result on 20 worlds: 15 have conflict in some form, 10 have killings, and the forms vary (one people duels, another brawls, another ambushes). Population held steady.
- **Hard-code fixes:**
  - The gang-theft cooldown is gone, with no regression without it.
  - Feasts follow plenty instead of a calendar day.
  - The remaining calendar-tied rules are listed in [AUDIT-HARDCODE.md](Anthro/docs/AUDIT-HARDCODE.md).
- **Tech audit** ([TECH-SETTLEMENT.md](Anthro/docs/TECH-SETTLEMENT.md)):
  - Every technology family is classified by kind of effect, and we've checked that each is actually used.
  - Resilience technologies (storage, drying, cooking, fishing) cut bad springs from 23 to between 1 and 9 without much raising numbers, which is what that kind of technology should do.
  - Weirs are now real structures at river spots. With the settling toolkit, camps stay at one site about 5 years on average, which matches the "semi-settled foraging village" form. Towns wait for farming.

**Found by the 150-year runs, and fixed**
1. **Rationing was being unlearned everywhere**, because summer meat spoilage taught "ration less". Now only stores that rotted while the family wasn't going short teach that. This is probably part of the 50–75-year population slump I saw.
2. **Peoples never split** (17 of 18 worlds stayed one people for 150 years). Splits now weigh how far camps' ways have drifted across all the learned customs, plus bad blood and time apart.

**Running now:** 20 worlds × 150 years with both fixes, about an hour. I'll check whether the slump eases and whether peoples split at a sensible rate. Then comes the light two-peoples check and the final readiness review against the checklist.

### Claude (Assistant)

The 150-year runs finished hours ago. My script that waited for them was stuck: one world died out at year 126, so it never reached year 150, and the script kept waiting for it. I've recorded that pitfall so it doesn't happen again.

Results with this morning's two fixes, 20 worlds over 150 years, against the same worlds before:

| | Before | After |
|---|---|---|
| Typical population at year 150 | 46 | 84 |
| Total population at year 150 | 1,667 | 2,106 |
| Worlds that died out | 0 | 1 (at year 126) |
| Worlds where the people split in two | 1 | 3 |
| Violent deaths | 286 | 378 |

- **Population slump:** populations still dip in the first 50 years, but now they keep growing afterwards instead of stalling around 40.
- **Splits:** peoples now occasionally split, which is a start but still rare.
- **Extinction:** one world dying out is in line with "a small band can die out without a god".
- **Rationing:** families still gradually stop rationing. It drifts down to about 0.3, from 0.6 at the start. Better than before, but not fixed.

**Yes, ready to compact.** The saved queue and `docs/PROGRESS.md` both have the current state.

**Next after compaction:**
1. Fix the rationing drift.
2. Rerun the tests. They last passed before today's speed and conflict changes.
3. A light check of two peoples sharing a map.
4. The remaining hard-coding items:
   - the marriage reviews still happen on fixed dates in the year;
   - the thresholds that make a camp split are still set by hand;
   - the elders' voice and hunting prestige can't yet change with experience.
5. A final readiness review against the checklist before the god layer.
