### User

This session is being continued from a previous conversation that ran out of context. The summary below covers the earlier portion of the conversation.

Summary:
**1. Primary Request and Intent**

**Project.** "Anthro" is an anthropological/biological evolution god-sim. It is a headless C# simulation (netstandard2.1) at `C:\Users\nars7\Downloads\Ideas and Thoughts\Anthro\`, planned for Unity later.

**The user's standing principles (in memory):**
- Everything emergent and organic; nothing scheduled, nothing global.
- World facts grounded in real data; hand-set numbers disclosed.
- For audits: findings first, then talk before changing.
- Don't commit to git unless asked.
- Realism doesn't need exact forager rates. What matters: natural, understood, not hard-coded.
- Without a god, survival should be hard and advancement slow. Bands can die out.
- The god speeds innovation, adds drama, and improves odds.

**Current overarching goal.** Get the base sim "all good" before starting the god layer. The user said: "I think the god layer starts once the base sim is all good are we there yet?"

**User decisions and feedback, 2026-10-07:**

*Discoveries*
- 3 per 40 years is fine.

*Violence*
- It should be common, because it adds drama. Not "10% chance of being an issue without the player."
- It should vary by culture, not by blanket rules.
- The user dislikes hard caps like the 3-exchange brawl limit.
- Duels vs brawls vs individual should be cultural.
- Theft: mostly sneaky. Assassination is possible. Desperation one-on-one.
- Raids must be worth it for many people to join; not every fight should spread to others.
- Personal drama (jealousy, leadership disputes) can lead to duels or brawls, culturally.

*Tech and settlements*
- Keep doing the tech audit ("i didnt mean to interrupt ur tech audit… make sure to still do that").
- Consider how techs affect settlements; towns/cities should maybe need baseline agriculture.
- Agriculture proper waits for the next milestone.

*Multi-people and long runs*
- Multi-people testing should be light, with no economics until the god layer.
- "Stable over generations" was explained: the 150-year runs.

*Speed*
- Speed first, since it speeds up testing.

*Plural marriage*
- Customs only for v1. Heritable mating psychology later, with accelerated evolution.

**Earlier asks completed this session:**
- Starvation fixes.
- Cross-camp sharing (refusal → grievance → raids).
- Plural marriage plus biology.
- Fight design (intent, exchanges, onlookers, peacemaking, routs, weapons, culture boundaries).
- #5 decision audit plus custom conversions.
- #6 settled-life progression.
- Tech review.
- Two-peoples test with kin camps.
- Discovery slowdown.
- Overnight reflection vs the original plan (`docs/REFLECTION.md`).
- Dense summary.

**2. Key Technical Concepts**

*Simulation structure*
- Region holds camps (`Simulation` + `Band`). `Tribe` = a people. `MaxTribes` = 5.
- Deterministic RNG.

*Decision-making*
- Fixed decision formula with learned inputs (`Expectations` topics):
  - `act:<act>:<rel>[:seen|unseen]`;
  - `lean:<season>`;
  - `wed:more`;
  - `bound:<relation>`;
  - `norm:*` — honour, peacemaking, rationing, demand, elders, hunting, sides, duel.
- These spread via `SocialLearning`, are inherited from the mother (`act:`, `wed:`, `bound:`, `norm:`), and are copied to kin camps.

*Fights (`Fights.cs`)*
- Aim is Display, Hurt or Kill.
- Severity = `Bound(rel)` + rage (anger/(anger+10) × (0.5+temper) × 0.9) + desperation × 0.2 − love × 0.8 − guilt + noise.
- AimOf thresholds: 0.6 and 1.5.
- Exchanges up to a 40 guard; fatigue +0.15 per exchange feeds fear.
- Resolve vs fear decides whether a fighter yields or flees.
- At injury ≥ 0.6 a fighter is down (immediately, in `Hit`); the laid-up can't join.
- Morale break; pursuit only by Kill-aim fighters (35%).
- Armed if hunting or Kill aim (×1.6).
- Onlookers join (weighted by `Sides`, needing severity ≥ 0.6) or peacemake. In gang fights, onlookers only defend or make peace.
- Catharsis after a fight (0.4; duel 0.8).
- `AfterKilling` shifts bound; honour gives following.
- Fight kinds: quarrel, duel, gang, raid, encounter, ambush.

*Settlement*
- `Investment`: heavy stores beyond carry, houses, tended grain, ochre.hand, weirs.
- `LostIfMoving` (each voter weighs their own family's loss; up to 4 trips on short moves); `LeaveBehindWhatCantBeCarried`.
- `Threat` after raids.
- Gatherings driven by urge plus plenty.
- Fish runs driven by water temperature.
- Weirs are structures at cells.

*Customs*
- `Customs.cs`: norm:rationing/demand/elders/hunting/sides/duel, plus the `ANTHRO_OFF` switchboard.

*Food*
- Cross-camp food asking (`Visit.ForFood`, `DoAsk` carryHome).

*Marriage*
- `Person.Spouses`, `Wed`/`Unwed`, `ConsiderAnotherSpouse` with biological stakes (`AtStake`), `LearnFromPluralMarriages`.

*Analysis tool (Demog, OLD scratchpad `ad250276…/scratchpad/Demog`)*
- Args: `seed years kin grants otherPeoples`.
- Reports:
  - fightkinds;
  - asked/gave/carried/abandoned;
  - campmoves, feasts, fights, peacemade, routs;
  - settled (days-at-site, weirs);
  - culture per people every 10 years.
- Env diagnostics: `ANTHRO_DEBUG_FIGHTS` (FIGHT/KILLBLOW lines), `ANTHRO_DEBUG_KILLS`, `ANTHRO_CHRON` regex, `ANTHRO_TRACE`, `ANTHRO_DEBUG_DEATHS`.

*Testing method*
- Compare builds on ≥20 worlds; 6–10 is too noisy.
- Ablations via the `ANTHRO_OFF` switches.

**3. Files and Code Sections**

All paths under `Anthro/Anthro.Sim/` unless stated.

*New files*
- **Fights.cs**:
  - Aim; Fighter; FightResult.
  - Bound/Honour/PeaceView; `SeedViolenceCulture` (inside N(0.5,0.35), outsider +N(0.5,0.3), honour N(0,0.35), peace N(0.5,0.25)); Severity.
  - `Fight(sideA, sideB, answers, overFood, inCamp, kind="quarrel", far=false)`.
  - RecordBlow (Far deeds use the unseen lesson); AfterKilling; DoAmbush (suspicion: kin add grievance 8 to a suspect; chronicle "found dead away from camp. Their family blame X, though it was Y"); DescribeFight (debug); NarrateFight.
- **Gatherings.cs**: urge-driven feast. `_gatherUrge += (1 + 0.5 if ritual)/DaysPerSeason`; needs ≥6 days of food; bond = 0.01 + ritual 0.03 + rites 0.01 + ochre.body 0.01 + story 0.005 + paint/figure 0.005 each, applied to pairs with affinity < 0.3; called daily.
- **Customs.cs**:
  - Topics `RationTopic`, `DemandTopic`, `EldersTopic`, `HuntingTopic`, `SidesTopic`, `DuelTopic`.
  - Accessors: `Rationing` (default 0.6), `Demand` (0.3), `Elders` (1), `HuntingPrestige` (0.4), `Sides` (0.4), `Duel` (0.2).
  - `SeedCustoms`; `Off(feature)` via `ANTHRO_OFF`.

*Modified files*
- **Family.cs**:
  - Spouses-aware Jealousy with AtStake.
  - ReconsiderMarriages (per spouse; LearnFromPluralMarriages in Spring).
  - Desire + ProviderStanding × 0.2.
  - ConsiderAnotherSpouse: shared × (male 0.6 : female 0.3); hub anticipates spouse sting × AtStake; mine ≥ 0.5.
  - LearnFromPluralMarriages: own children via KidsLastYear; onlookers judge by sex.
  - ConsiderLeaving(p, drawnTo, strain, partner); Separate is plural-aware.
- **People/Person.cs**: `Spouses`, `WedOn`, `Partner` (get-only), `Married`, `Wed`/`Unwed`, `LastRallyDay` (now unused by Decider).
- **Simulation.cs**:
  - FatherOfNext; Investment property; Threat/LastThreatDay; DaysHere.
  - LostIfMoving / CarryOnMove / LeaveBehindWhatCantBeCarried(hours).
  - YearStats: GuestAsks/Gifts/Food, Abandoned, FightsBy/FightDeathsBy.
  - `_healers` (forage.lore ×1.6 care).
  - Rationing learning: WinterLow < 0.5 → toward 1 (0.1). Unlearn only if `p.WinterLow > 0.8 && Home.StoresSpoiledRecent > members*2` → toward 0 (0.05).
  - StoresSpoiledRecent EMA 0.99; pot.pit care 0.9; kill debug; Gathering() called daily.
- **Society.cs**:
  - DoAttack → duel chance `(Duel(a)+Duel(t))/2` → Fight kind duel (inCamp false) or quarrel.
  - DoAsk carryHome; violence seed N(−2.5, 1.0); unseen attack lesson seeded at violence + 1; SeedViolenceCulture and SeedCustoms called.
  - Witness skips camp onlookers for Far deeds; `OutAlone(p)` (Forage/Fish/GatherWood/Trap).
  - ConfrontValue(p, t, backing) overload; allocation-free Tell (gossip).
  - InheritSocialExpectations includes `bound:` and `norm:`.
- **JointActs.cs**:
  - Caches `FreshCaches`/`AdultsNow`/`MemberById`.
  - RallyValue(p, h, hunger) single-pass, plus `(Sides(p)-0.4)*0.5`; FamilyHunger.
  - DoRally → Fight gang (no defenders = prevail); taking cools anger 0.6.
  - Defends fear = raiders.Sum(Might)/Might(p) × 0.25 × Safety.
  - DoWarRaid → Fight raid, sets target.LastThreatDay; encounters → Fight encounter.
- **Activities/Decider.cs**:
  - Foresight/LeanDays/Keeping/MeatWillKeep; hunger worry term (when Income < need).
  - Trips sized to daylight (gathering ≥3 h, hunting ≥4 h).
  - Ask/take drive = hunger × 1.5 (no baseline); ask gate `hunger > 0.3*(1-0.6*demand) && DaysOfFood < 2`.
  - Hunt weight uses HuntingPrestige; preserve option includes SpoiledRecent.
  - Anger block iterates p.Grievance via MemberById; Ambush option for targets OutAlone (hate > 1, unseen expectation).
  - Rally has no cooldown; Visit has a ForFood flag.
- **Activities/SocialActs.cs**: `Ambush` activity (travel, DoAmbush if still OutAlone, walk back).
- **Activities/Visit.cs**: ForFood, pack, asking hearths every 3 h, Unpack on return.
- **Activities/CampActivities.cs** (Eat):
  - Eat 1.5× only if perishableDays > 2 or DaysOfFood > stretch + 3.
  - Rationing scaled by custom strictness.
- **Activities/FieldActivities.cs**:
  - Gather hours capped by daylight.
  - Nuts: raw 0.75, forage.nuts 1.0, pound ×1.3, mortar ×1.15.
  - Grain: slab ×1.3, pound 0.9, none 0.7 (with ANTHRO_OFF "nuts" restoring the old values).
- **Activities/Fishing.cs**:
  - `Efficiency(p, run, spot)`: weir works only at a spot with `Weir ≥ 0.5`.
  - Building +0.08 × (0.5 + str) per hour (returns while < 0.5); maintenance +0.02; fishing hours capped by daylight.
- **Activities/Hunt.cs**: Outlook struct/OutlookOf (speed); canEat includes the frozen-meat store shortfall.
- **Activities/HearthActivities.cs**: Preserve rate 15/30.
- **World/Cell.cs**:
  - WaterTemp; run by warming/cooling window, with the "fishrun" switch.
  - `Weir` decays 0.003/day.
- **People/Household.cs**: Income, StoresSpoiledRecent.
- **People/Deeds.cs**: `Far`.
- **Inventing.cs**: DiscoveryRate 1e-7.
- **Region.cs**: AddKinCamps (other peoples get kin camps).
- **Tribe.cs**: RiftWith uses `Math.Max(household, ways)`, where `ways = min(1, mean |Δ| over _cultureTopics / 0.33)`; `_cultureTopics` covers the bound, norm and wed:more topics.

*Tests*
- **Anthro.Tests/SimulationTests.cs**: added `MarriagesAreMutualAndAmongTheLiving` and `PeoplesStartWithTheirOwnCultures`. 15 tests passed, last run before today's speed/conflict/weir changes.

*Docs*
- **docs/**:
  - `AUDIT-DECISIONS.md` (new; conversions marked done);
  - `AUDIT-HARDCODE.md` (additions 2026-10-07: fixed items plus still-open list);
  - `REFLECTION.md` (true vs drifted vs PLANNING.md; god layer biggest gap);
  - `TECH-SETTLEMENT.md` (settlement forms; effect kinds; gaps);
  - `PROGRESS.md` (sections: overnight, final validation, "2026-10-07 day: base-sim readiness").

*Memory*
- `anthro-overnight-queue.md` (current state, next steps, gotchas);
- `anthro-balance-target.md` (updated: survival not easy; discovery slow; violence OK if logical; city-builder arc);
- `anthro-realism-not-exactness.md`;
- `anthro-sim-before-god.md`;
- MEMORY.md index updated.

**4. Errors and fixes**

*Violence iterations*
- 15-dead pile-ups → onlooker restraint.
- Hunger driving kill intent → desperation weight 0.2.
- 929 gang thefts a year → taking cools anger (a cooldown was added, then removed after the user disliked hard caps).
- Outnumbered defenders now give way.
- Killing blows on the already-wounded → down at 0.6 immediately; laid-up can't fight.
- Rage scaled by /(anger+10).
- In-group bound seed 0.5.
- Catharsis after fights.
- 3-exchange cap (the user disliked it) → exhaustion-based endings.

*Population regression*
- v57 halved population. Traced via 20-world ablations to demand sharing: 1.2 h/day spent asking.
- Fixed by need-only drive and the hunger-threshold gate (v61).

*Other errors*
- Moving abandoned stores → multi-trip carry plus each voter weighing their family's loss.
- Demog chronicle missed mid-year camps → `Watch()` daily.
- Misread "dogs" column (it was moves) → corrected to the user.
- Bash heredoc quoting broke C# → wrote Python patch files.
- C# 9 nested interpolation in the debug line → helper `DescribeFight`.
- `Rng.Next` missing → `Rng.Range(0, 1.0)`.
- Deconstruction type inference error → explicit variables.
- awk parse of Y100+ lines garbled the long-run series → Python parsing.
- The wait loop hung about 4 h because world 46 went extinct at Y126 and never reached Y150. The user asked "its been 4hrs everything ok"; I fixed and reported. Gotcha saved: wait loops must not require all worlds to reach the last year.

*Analysis mistakes*
- Plural biology initially didn't change entry decisions → stakes moved into the decision.
- Rationing unlearned by summer spoilage → only rotted stores teach it.
- Schisms never happened → full culture distance in the rift.

**5. Problem Solving**

*Solved*
- Starvation causes; demand regression; fight lethality and logic.
- Conflict now cultural: in v63/v65, 15 of 20 worlds have conflict and 10 have killings, in varied forms.
- Speed +18% (34 s → 28 s per 10 years).
- Tech usage verified: resilience techs cut bad springs from 23 to 1–9.
- Weirs/settledness: tech-start camps stay about 5 years at a site, about 90 weirs.
- Long-run slump eased: median pop at Y150 is 84, up from 46. 1 of 20 worlds extinct; 3 of 20 have 2 peoples.

*Ongoing*
- Rationing still drifts down to about 0.3.
- Gang-theft volume is very high in some worlds.
- Tech-start worlds' population is lower than the old v37t build.
- Settlement only reaches the semi-settled level (by design until agriculture).

### Claude (Assistant)

The rationing custom was being pushed down by a flawed lesson rule. I fixed the rule, but the bigger finding is that rationing barely affects anyone yet, because most families have almost nothing stored to ration. Tests pass (15/15). Other checks are still running.

**What rationing is.** Every family has a custom between 0 and 1: how hard they cut meals when the food at home won't last the lean stretch they expect. It starts at a random value around 0.6 for each people and changes as families learn from experience. Talk and inheritance spread it but don't push it up or down.

**Why it drifted down to about 0.3.** I counted every lesson in 20 worlds over 50 years:
- **Pushed up:** anyone who got thin in a season moved toward rationing harder. That happened 23,000 times.
- **Pushed down:** anyone well fed whose family's stores had rotted moved toward rationing less. That happened 85,000 times.
- **Spurious lessons:** 68% of the downward lessons came from families that never had to budget that season. Their spoilage said nothing about rationing, but it still taught them to ration less.
- **The math:** with those two flows, the custom settles at 0.35, exactly where it ended up (0.32). So it wasn't really a drift; it was a wrong lesson that balanced at 0.35.

**The fix (in `Customs.cs`):** a season only teaches rationing if the family actually had to budget its food.
- **They budgeted and still ran out and went hungry:** they learn to ration harder.
- **They budgeted and their stores rotted anyway:** they learn they pinched for nothing. The lesson is as strong as the share of the store that rotted. So with good storage, little rots and rationing pays off; where food rots fast, it doesn't.

The custom now holds around 0.55, varying from 0.30 to 0.70 between worlds. The old rule is still available as a switch for comparison.

**Your question: is the learning correct, and is something else broken?** Mostly the latter. Rationing barely matters yet:
- **Too little to ration:** families that budget hold back only 3–5 days' food per person over a whole season, out of about 80 needed.
- **Most run out anyway:** 74–83% of the families budgeting in winter and spring run out regardless.
- **Stores are very unequal:** going through winter, the typical family has about 2 weeks of keeping food per person, while the top tenth have 8 months' worth.
- **Ration hard vs never:** forcing every family to always ration hard, compared with never rationing, gave somewhat fewer starvation deaths (1,597 vs 1,731) but also lower population (709 vs 823). That difference is about the size of the normal world-to-world variation.

**The world rule I think is wrong:** stored nuts and dried food rot at the same rate in deep winter as in summer, about 30% a season without storage technology. Families with big stores lose more to rot in winter (27 days' food per person) than everyone saves by rationing. Rot should slow in the cold. I haven't changed this; do you want me to make spoilage depend on temperature?

**Other changes since you left, all still being tested:**
- **Two peoples (40 years, 20 worlds):** both peoples survive in 9 of 20 worlds. Conflict is common, but gang thefts are extreme, up to about 100 a year in some small camps.
- **Likely cause of the gang thefts:** people only got restless enough to leave a camp when it was crowded. Feuds, hunger and overwork never made anyone want to leave, so feuding families in small camps kept robbing each other. Moving away is how foragers mostly end feuds. Now any discontent can make someone restless.
- **Marriage reviews:** these no longer happen on a fixed day each season. People take stock of a marriage after a hungry season, or when a child is born in camp and they've had none together in years. Plural marriages are judged at births and in lean times.
- **Noise check:** population totals have fallen across today's builds (953 → 692 on the same 20 worlds). To see whether that's real or chance, I'm rerunning three of today's builds on 20 fresh worlds. That takes about 1.5 hours, because everything shares the CPU.

Notes are in `docs/PROGRESS.md`. I'll report the results when they finish.

### Claude (Assistant)

Results from the 20 fresh worlds are in, and they change one thing I told you:

| Build | Total population at year 50 | Starvation deaths |
|---|---|---|
| Old rationing lessons | 1,018 | 2,046 |
| New rationing lessons | 882 | 1,935 |
| New rationing lessons + new marriage reviews | 885 | 2,031 |

- **The marriage-review change makes no difference to population.**
- **The rationing fix costs about 13% of population.** It did on both sets of 20 worlds (953 → 805 before, 1,018 → 882 now), so it isn't noise. Keeping the custom near 0.55 instead of 0.3 means fewer people.

**Why.** In this world, cutting meals harder trades a few starvation deaths for thinner mothers and fewer births, and stores are too small for the trade to pay off. The old rule reached a better value (about 0.3) through the wrong reasoning. My new rule teaches "ration harder" whenever a family runs out, but most families run out no matter what, because their stores are tiny. It also never counts the hunger that rationing itself causes.

I'd rather you choose here than me tuning toward an outcome:
- **A. Keep the new rule.** It's a natural human inference ("we ran out, we should have pinched harder"), even when that's wrong. People holding a costly custom is realistic and gives the god something to change.
- **B. Also make families learn from rationing's cost.** If they budgeted and still ran out, the pinching only made them hungry sooner, so the lesson leans toward rationing less. The custom would then settle lower in worlds where stores are small.
- **C. Fix the world rule first.** Make stored food rot more slowly in the cold, so families carry real stores through winter, then see where rationing settles. I lean towards C, with A or B decided afterwards. It's the root cause, and it's grounded: mould and insects slow sharply in the cold.

The feud and restlessness test and the event-driven marriage reviews are still running. I'll report those when they finish.

### Claude (Assistant)

The big win while you were out: the runaway gang thefts are fixed. On the rationing, I need a decision from you. The 15 tests pass on the current version.

**Rationing, short version** (the full explanation is in my earlier message):
- **Why it drifted down:** the old rule taught families to ration less whenever their stores rotted, even in seasons when they never had to budget. That outweighed the push up, and the two balanced at about 0.35, which is where it settled.
- **My fix:** only seasons where a family actually had to budget teach anything. The custom now holds around 0.55, varying by world.
- **But it costs about 13% of population,** consistently across 40 worlds, because rationing barely helps anyone. Families have almost no stores, so most run out anyway, and the pinching just means thinner mothers and fewer births.
- **The broken world rule:** stored food rots as fast in deep winter as in summer.

**Your call:**
- **A.** Keep my new rule. People believe "we should have pinched harder" even when it's wrong.
- **B.** Also teach families the cost of pinching, so the custom settles lower where stores are small.
- **C.** First make stored food rot more slowly in the cold, then see where rationing settles. This is the one I'd pick.

**Gang thefts were an hourly loop.** In one world, families took each other's food by force 3,836 times in 12 years, 500 of them in one autumn, mostly when nobody was hungry. Two causes:
1. **No reason needed:** the learned sense that "taking pays" and the side-taking custom applied with no hunger or anger behind them. So after a few successes, people raided while full and at peace. Now there has to be a motive (hunger, or anger at that family), and the side-taking custom only affects people joining someone else's gang.
2. **No fear after a beating:** the gang's leader ignored their own wounds and barely weighed how strong the family was. Gangs that were driven off tried again within the hour. Now the leader weighs the family's strength against their own, the way defenders already do, plus their own wounds. A beating keeps them off for days, with no cooldown rule.

20 worlds each:

| | Gang fights | Violent deaths | Population |
|---|---|---|---|
| One people, 50 years, before | 15,165 | 305 | 692 |
| One people, 50 years, after | 614 | 122 | 871 |
| Two peoples, 40 years, before | 32,192 | 547 | 1,090 |
| Two peoples, 40 years, after | 3,049 | 359 | 1,503 |

Violence is still common, just driven by actual reasons now:
- **Two peoples:** killings in 18 of 20 worlds. Raids between the peoples nearly doubled (564 → 979), so they now fight each other more and themselves less.
- **One people:** killings in 12 of 20 worlds. The number of fights varies a lot by culture, from 0 to 351 per world over 50 years.

**Other changes:**
- **Restlessness:** any discontent (feuds, hunger, long work days, crowding) can now make people want to leave a camp, not just crowding. On its own this made only a small difference.
- **Marriage reviews:** these now happen only when something prompts them, with no calendar and no dice.
  - A spouse takes stock after a season that left them thin.
  - A couple with no children after 4 years takes stock when a baby is born in camp.
  - The camp judges a plural marriage when a child is born into it or the household goes hungry.
  
  This made no difference to population.
- **Two peoples sharing a map:** checked lightly as you asked, with no economics. Both peoples are still alive after 40 years in 8 of 20 worlds.

**Still open:** the hand-set split thresholds and the weir building and decay rates. The split thresholds are based on real forager work hours and band sizes, so I've documented them rather than changed them. The elders' voice and hunting prestige still don't change with experience. I'll propose learning signals for those before building them. After that comes the readiness review.

Everything is written up in `docs/PROGRESS.md` and `docs/AUDIT-HARDCODE.md`.

### Claude (Assistant)

My advice: fix the world rule first, then fix the lesson so it can push the custom either way. Here's the reasoning.

**What rationing is for.** Rationing only helps when there's food to spread over a lean stretch. Right now there mostly isn't:

| Fact from the 20-world runs | What it means |
|---|---|
| Going into winter, the typical family has about 2 weeks of stored food per person | There's almost nothing to ration |
| 74–83% of families who budget in winter or spring run out anyway | Pinching harder wouldn't have saved them |
| Stored food rots about 30% a season, as fast in winter as in summer | Keeping food through winter is self-defeating |
| A higher rationing custom gives slightly fewer starvation deaths but about 13% lower population | Pinching thins mothers and costs births, and buys little in return |

So the problem has two layers: a world rule that keeps stores tiny, and a lesson rule that misreads what happened.

**Step 1: make stored food rot more slowly in the cold.** This is the root cause, and it can be grounded in real biology rather than a number I pick:
- Mould, bacteria and insects slow down sharply in the cold, roughly halving their activity for every 10 °C cooler. Near freezing, stored food barely spoils.
- That's why real foragers' winter caches work: acorn pits in California and Japan lasted a year or more, and dried fish kept for months.
- The rule would be: today's rot rate stays as the rate at a mild temperature, slows as it gets colder, and storage technology still cuts it further on top.

What I'd expect:
- **Bigger winter stores.** Families should carry real stores into winter, so rationing finally has something to work with.
- **More settling.** Heavy stores tie people to a place, which is the settling path you wanted.
- **Possibly too easy.** Survival could get easier than your balance target. I'd check that and report rather than retune anything.

**Step 2: make the lesson fair in both directions.** People should learn from what they could actually see happen in a season when they had to budget:

| What happened | Lesson | How strong |
|---|---|---|
| They had real stores, budgeted, and still ran out | Pinch harder | By how much they had to spread at the start; with nothing to spread, no lesson |
| They went hungry pinching and still had food left at the end | Pinched too hard | By how much was left over |
| Stores rotted while they pinched | Pinched for nothing | By the share of stores that rotted (as now) |
| They budgeted and came through the season with nothing left over | It worked | No change |

- **What's new:** an "ease off" lesson for pinching that wasn't needed, and the "pinch harder" lesson now scaled by whether pinching could have helped at all.
- **Why it matters:** too strict shows up as leftovers and rot; too loose shows up as running out despite having stores. The custom then settles wherever each world's food actually puts it, so it isn't fixed at any particular level.
  - **Little to store:** it should drift low, like the old rule did, but for the right reasons.
  - **Good storage and a hard winter:** it should rise.
  - **Over time:** as storage technology spreads, it should rise with it.

That gives you variation between cultures, and something meaningful for the god to change later. For example, teaching pit storage would change rationing through its effects, not by flipping a number.

**Why not just A or B on their own?**
- **A** (keep the current rule) leaves a custom that settles at a costly level because the lesson is miscounted, not because the world calls for it. That's not the kind of costly custom you want for drama.
- **B** alone would fix the lesson but leave rationing pointless in nearly every world, since there's still nothing to ration.

**One thing to keep separate.** Stores are very unequal: the top tenth of families have about 8 months' food per person going into winter while the typical family has 2 weeks. Rich families losing food to rot while neighbours starve is a sharing problem, not a rationing one. I'd leave it alone for now. Better winter storage will make it stand out more, which suits drama and a god's influence later.

**How I'd test it:**
1. Build step 1 and compare 20 worlds over 50 years against the current build. I'd watch winter stores, how often families run out, starvation, births, population, how long camps stay put, and whether survival is still hard.
2. Add step 2 and run 20 more. Check that rationing now differs by world and settles at a level that doesn't cost population.
3. Re-check on 20 fresh worlds, since we know smaller samples can't detect effects like these.

**Hand-set numbers to disclose:** the base rot rate (already hand-set) and the "halves every 10 °C" figure. The second is a standard biological rule of thumb, but it is still a chosen value.

Shall I go ahead with steps 1 and 2? After that I'd move on to the still-open items (the elders' voice and hunting prestige learning from experience, and the weir rates), then the readiness review.

### Claude (Assistant)

Both rationing changes are built, and I'm testing them now on 20 worlds over 50 years. Results in about 1.5 hours.

**Step 1: stored food rots more slowly in the cold.** Rot now halves for every 10 °C cooler.
- **Summer** (about 20 °C): unchanged.
- **Spring and autumn** (about 8 °C): rot is a bit under half of what it was.
- **Winter** (about −4 °C): rot is about a fifth of what it was, so roughly 7% of a store over a winter instead of 30%.
- **Storage technology** still reduces rot further on top of this.

**Step 2: the lesson is now fair both ways.**
- **Pinch harder:** when a family ran out and went hungry, scaled by how much they had to spread. With almost nothing stored, they don't blame the custom.
- **Ease off:** when they pinched and still have more than the lean time ahead needs. Food kept from autumn for winter doesn't count as a surplus.
- **Ease off:** when stores rotted while they pinched, as before.
- **No change:** when they budgeted and came through fine.

**The three versions being compared:** the current build, the new lesson alone, and both changes together. I'm also recording each family's stores every season, so I can show whether winter stores grow, whether running out drops, and where the custom settles. I'll also check whether survival is still hard, as your balance target needs.

**The next open items, and what I'd propose for them.** I'll wait for your OK before building either.

- **Elders' voice.** Right now the custom only changes how much an elder's vote counts when the camp decides whether to move.
  - **Proposed signal:** people judge the outcome. A season after a move, they compare how well they eat at the new site with the old one.
  - **If the elders' side won the vote:** a good move raises respect for elders and a bad one lowers it.
  - **If the elders were outvoted and the move went badly:** people learn "we should have listened".
  - **Effect over time:** peoples whose old people carry useful memory of places learn to defer to them.
- **Hunting prestige.** This decides whether people hunt for food or for standing.
  - **Proposed signal:** what actually feeds the camp. Each season, people lean toward hunters' prestige by the share of the camp's food that came from hunting.
  - **Effect over time:** big-game peoples come to prize hunters. Fishing and nut-gathering peoples drift away from it, so hunting becomes less of a status game there.
- **Weir building and decay rates:** I'd leave these hand-set and disclosed. They're physical facts (about 12 person-hours to build; gone in about a year without upkeep), not behaviour.

Shall I build the elders and hunting signals once the rationing results are in?

### Claude (Assistant)

The rationing results are in, and they're good. The elders and hunting test has just started, so its results aren't in yet.

20 worlds, 50 years, same worlds for all three versions:

| | Current build | New lesson only | New lesson + slower cold rot |
|---|---|---|---|
| Total population at year 50 | 871 | 1,096 | **1,429** |
| Typical world's population | 40 | 52 | **67** (about where they started) |
| Starvation deaths | 2,005 | 1,911 | 2,072 |
| Rationing custom at year 50 | 0.57 | 0.15 | **0.33** (worlds range 0.12–0.49) |
| Stored food rotted per person over winter | 28 | 29 | **6** |

- **Population is up about 64% and survival is still hard.** Starvation deaths didn't change, and the typical world only holds steady over 50 years. Bands still struggle; they just waste less.
- **The lesson works both ways now.** With the new lesson alone, rationing falls to 0.15, because with fast rot there's little worth budgeting. With slower cold rot as well, it settles around 0.33 and varies by world. It's still falling slowly at year 50, so it may settle a bit lower.
- **Worlds with the biggest stores ration least** (the link is moderate, −0.34). Families with plenty end seasons with surpluses, so they learn they pinched for nothing. That's sensible: rationing pays when stores are tight relative to need, not when food is abundant or absent.

**The next bottleneck: families don't store enough.** Slower rot didn't make the typical family store more:
- **Stores going into winter:** about 2 weeks of food per person, the same as before.
- **Running out:** about 3 in 4 families who have to budget in winter still run out.

The gain mostly came from families with big stores no longer losing them to rot. So the limit is now how much people choose to put by in autumn, which is foresight and work time, not spoilage. I'd treat that as its own question rather than tune it now. It's also natural ground for the god layer: teaching people to store is exactly the kind of help a god could give.

I'm keeping both rationing changes. Next I'll report the elders and hunting results, then do the readiness review.

### Claude (Assistant)

We're almost there: 9 of the 10 checklist items now pass. The one failing item is that no people ever splits into a new people, so the "3–5 peoples" part of your player scale is still missing. The 15 tests pass.

**What changed since the rationing fix**
- **Elders' voice:** now learns from how the moves the old pushed turned out. It has no effect on population and varies by world, from 0.45 to 1.56 by year 50.
- **Hunting prestige:** I tried tying it to how much of the camp's food comes from hunting, then removed it. It fed on itself: lower prestige meant less hunting, so less meat, so lower prestige. Within 50 years it fell everywhere, cost 27% of population, and doubled gang thefts because people were hungrier. Test runs with it switched off confirmed the cause. It stays purely cultural, passed down and spread by talk, which also makes it a natural lever for the god.
- **Weir building and decay:** left as fixed physical facts, and written down as such.

**Readiness review** (20 worlds over 150 years, 20 two-peoples worlds over 40 years, 10 tech-start worlds over 40 years):

| # | Requirement | Status |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Survival hard without a god | ✅ The typical world grows slowly, from 70 to 136 people in 150 years. None died out, though 2 are close (8 and 21 people), and starvation is still common. It's slightly easier than before (1 of 20 died out then) |
| 2 | Slow advance without help | ✅ The typical world knows 4 discoveries by year 40 and 10–11 by year 150 |
| 3 | Technology leads to bigger, more settled camps | ✅ Up to the forager ceiling. Tech-start worlds have main camps of 40–110 people that stay about 6 years at a site, with 135 weirs. Towns wait for agriculture |
| 4 | Food and starvation understood | ✅ Rationing is explained and fixed |
| 5 | Ecology stable | ✅ Deer, boar, rabbit and wolf numbers hold or rise. Deer are hunted down in 2 crowded worlds, which is realistic |
| 6 | Cultures vary, for logical reasons | ✅ With one people, there are killings in 14 of 20 worlds. With two peoples, killings in 18 of 20 and real raids between the peoples. Gang thefts are down from 32,000 to 3,000 |
| 7 | Nothing hard-coded that should be learned | ✅ The hand-set numbers left are physical facts, documented in `docs/AUDIT-HARDCODE.md` |
| 8 | Player scale of 3–5 peoples | ❌ **No people split in 150 years (0 of 20, it was 3 of 20)** |
| 9 | Stable over generations | ✅ No runaway growth or slow collapse. Cultures don't drift to extremes, except rationing |
| 10 | Fast enough | ✅ About 3 seconds per simulated year at around 70 people |

**Why #8 fails.** A camp breaks away when bad blood with its own people, years without meeting them, and different customs add up past a threshold.
- **Less bad blood:** the gang-theft loop was producing most of the grudges between families, and it's gone now.
- **No time apart:** camps stay close and in touch.
- **Customs drift together:** across camps of the same people they move in step instead of apart.

So none get there. I'd think it through with you before changing it, because it's a design question. Should splits come mostly from distance and isolation (realistic: peoples form over centuries), from feuds, or from leadership disputes? There's also a practical option: start the god game with several peoples already on the map, which the sim already supports.

**Two smaller open points:**
- **Storing:** families put little food by, typically about 2 weeks' worth going into winter, so 3 in 4 of those who have to budget still run out. That's realistic for foragers, and a natural thing for a god to improve.
- **Rationing:** it keeps sliding, down to 0.12 by year 150. That's correct for this world, where small stores make pinching pointless, but it means the custom barely matters until storage improves.

How do you want to handle peoples splitting? Once that's settled, I think the base sim is ready for the god layer.

### Claude (Assistant)

**Rationing with storage tech:** no, it doesn't go up. It falls.

| Year 40, same 10 worlds | Rationing custom |
|---|---|
| No head-start technology | 0.39 average |
| Head start with storage, drying, weirs and huts | 0.26 average |

This is my earlier prediction proving wrong, not a bug. Rationing only pays in a middle zone: enough food stored to spread over a lean time, but not plenty.
- **Without technology,** most families have too little stored for pinching to save them.
- **With storage technology at rich sites,** families come out of lean seasons with food left over, so they learn they pinched for nothing.

That's believable: the rich storing peoples in the record are known for feasting, not austerity. It should also make good drama. A people that has unlearned rationing gets caught by a truly hard winter, runs out despite real stores, and learns to ration again. I haven't confirmed that rebound in a run yet. I'd check it once the god layer can bring on a hard winter.

**Are we ready for the god layer?** Yes. With one people fine for now, all of the 10 readiness items that matter pass, and the tests pass. Storing little food and the low rationing are realistic and natural things for a god to influence, not blockers. I've noted your decision on peoples so I don't push splits again.

**What's left before v1 goes into Unity.** Your first milestone, as planned, is:
- one band of about 30 in a small valley;
- about 10 discoveries;
- 4–5 god powers;
- belief forming from the god's actions;
- headless first, then Unity with simple shapes.

The base sim covers the first two. What's left, in order:

1. **God layer core (the big one)**:
   - **Favor and belief:** favor comes from prayer, rituals and offerings. Belief caps the favor pool. Faith fades without signs, doubt spreads by talk, and reputation runs from kind to cruel.
   - **Prayers as requests:** answering one grows belief, ignoring it grows doubt. Coincidences earn credit or blame for free.
   - **4–5 powers:** for example, an omen, placing animals or resources, weather for a season, a nudge at a life fork, a blessed birth.
   - **Losing:** the "last ember" chances, then the god fades, or the people die out.
2. **Belief and religion forming from the god's actions.** People interpret what the god does, often wrongly. This runs on the same learning system everything else uses, as beliefs about the god.
3. **Prophet with a menu of options.** The prophet gets the message right; listeners drift from it. Free typing with an LLM translator comes later.
4. **A starting setup for the milestone:** one band of about 30 in a small valley, checked against the balance target, which is that it can die out without help.
5. **The boundary between the sim and Unity:**
   - a read-only snapshot each frame (people, where they are, what they're doing, camps, animals, weather);
   - a stream of events for notifications and the chronicle;
   - a way to send god actions in;
   - time controls (pause, speed up);
   - save and load.

   I haven't checked how well the sim tracks individual people's positions within a camp, which Unity will need for rendering. That's the first thing to look at here.
6. **Tests for the god layer,** like the 15 existing ones, so the god's effects can be measured. For example: do answered prayers raise belief, and does a god improve the odds of survival?
7. **Then Unity v1 with simple shapes.**

Your timeline note says the headless code was meant to take about 2 weeks. Even so, the god layer is the biggest piece of real design left, and parts of it are still open in `PLANNING.md`: limits on the big powers, whether the god's skill tree is visible, rival gods. I'd begin with a short design pass, where I propose how favor, belief and prayers work in sim terms and you approve it before I build. That keeps to your "talk before changing" rule.

Shall I write that design pass?

---

### Claude (Assistant)

---

### Claude (Assistant)

No. "Too little for pinching to save them" means the pinching only delays the moment they run out. Most of those families don't die.

**What happens to a family that runs out.** Going into winter, the typical family has about 2 weeks of stored food per person, and the lean stretch they face is often 2 to 3 months. Pinching can cut meals to half at most, so 2 weeks of food stretches to about 4. They still run out partway through. After that they live day to day on what they can forage, fish or hunt, and they ask or take from other families and camps. So they run thin, and the weakest (small children, the old, the sick) are the ones who die. But most families that run out come through.

**On your point: you're right, and the sim already does this.** Pinching trades survival now for children later. Hungry, thin mothers conceive less, and that's real biology: foragers have fewer births after lean seasons. When I forced everyone to always ration hard, versus never rationing:
- **Starvation deaths:** fewer, 1,597 vs 1,731.
- **Births:** fewer too, so population ended lower, 709 vs 823.

So pinching does save some lives. It costs more in children who were never born, at least when stores are as small as they are now. That's a real trade-off, not a bug.

**The part that matters for the design: people never learn from the births.** Nobody can see a child who wasn't conceived. They learn only from what they can see:
- "We ran out and went hungry even though we had food to spread" → pinch harder.
- "We pinched and still had plenty left over, or it rotted" → ease off.

So the population cost isn't what pushes rationing down. I only showed it as a result. A people whose stores are in the middle zone, enough to spread but not plenty, can keep a strict custom that saves lives but means fewer kids, and the sim won't talk them out of it. That's the cultural choice you're describing, and it's allowed now.

The earlier 13% population drop was a different thing. That came from the miscounted lesson holding rationing high in worlds where it saved almost nobody, because there was so little to spread.

If it's useful, I can break down who actually dies of starvation (children, the old, or adults) and how often a family that runs out loses someone. That would show how harsh running out really is in the sim right now.

### Claude (Assistant)

Both ideas work, and they work best together. I'd use them like this.

**What has been eating the budget:** mostly the long test loops. Every change needed 20–60 simulation runs, each waited on and then picked apart. That's legwork, and another agent can do it just as well as me.

**Suggested split**

| Who | Does what |
|---|---|
| **Me (Opus), sparingly** | Design decisions, writing precise specs, reviewing diffs and run results, judging whether something is natural or hard-coded |
| **Sonnet** (switch in the model picker) | Implementing from my specs inside Claude Code. It reads the same memory and docs and costs less per task |
| **Antigravity** | The heavy runs: building, 20-world comparisons, test runs, pulling numbers out of the results. Also a second implementer for well-specified pieces |

**What makes the handoff work:**
1. **Use git.** The project isn't a git repo yet, and with two or three agents editing the same files, you need to be able to see and undo what each one changed. I'd run `git init` and make one commit of the current state as a baseline. You've said no commits unless you ask, so that's your call.
2. **A shared brief for the other agents.** Antigravity can't see my memory. I'd write an `AGENTS.md` in the project with:
   - your principles (emergent, nothing scheduled or global, grounded numbers disclosed, talk before changing);
   - how to build and run Demog: the arguments, the tech-start setup, the `ANTHRO_OFF` switches;
   - the testing rules (20+ worlds; wait loops must not require every world to reach the last year);
   - the report format to hand back to me.
3. **Specs small enough to check.** For Phase 1, I write `docs/GOD-LAYER.md` (the design) and break it into numbered tasks. Each task says which files to touch, what to reuse, which switch it gets, and what result proves it works ("over 20 worlds, the no-god baseline matches v80"). Then I only spend budget reviewing.

**For tonight, with what's left of my budget:**
- Write the design pass (`docs/GOD-LAYER.md`) for you to approve.
- Write `AGENTS.md` and the Phase 1 task list.
- Git setup if you want it.

Sonnet or Antigravity then starts on task 1. I'd skip running any simulations myself.

Should I set up git and write those three documents?

  - **Belief:** it lives in people as learned beliefs (whether the god is real, whether it answers, what it thinks of each act). It spreads by talk, is inherited, and fades without signs.
  - **Acts of belief:** praying, offerings and rites are things people choose to do. They're the only source of favor; nothing trickles in on its own.
  - **Prayers:** each one is a request that stays open while the need lasts. A need met by chance still earns credit.
  - **Five powers:** omen, bounty, weather, blessed birth, nudge at a life fork.
  - **Misinterpretation:** people tie a striking event to a recent deed they remember. For example, "the sky burned after Ka took food" becomes "the god punishes taking". That's the seed of religion.
  - **Endings:** reputation, last embers, and the god fading.
- **[docs/PHASE1-TASKS.md](docs/PHASE1-TASKS.md):** tasks T0–T8 in order, each with the files to touch and a clear test that it's done.

### Claude (Assistant)

Gemini built a lot overnight, all nine tasks, and the structure is good, but it isn't ready to merge. I checked the risky parts myself and ran one direct test.

**What's solid:**
- **Clean structure:** the god code sits in its own folder, every part has an off-switch, and the power commands run in a fixed order.
- **Mostly as designed:** prayers, the five powers, credit and blame from remembered deeds, offerings, rites, last embers and fading, and bot gods for testing.
- **Tests:** 15 new tests, all passing.
- **Git discipline:** one branch per task and a report for each. `master` is untouched.

**Problems:**
1. **Switching the god off doesn't restore the base sim.** With the god layer turned off, seed 41 should give exactly the same history as the baseline run. It diverges from year 1. Two changes that aren't behind the switch touch the base game:
   - every person now remembers their own deeds, which changes gossip for everyone;
   - seeding belief uses up random numbers, which shifts every random draw that follows.
2. **"No regressions" was claimed without evidence.** Adding belief alone, which shouldn't change behaviour at all, moved total population from 1,327 to 941, and the report still said "no regressions". Most of this is probably the random-number shift from problem 1, but nobody measured the normal world-to-world variation to tell the two apart.
3. **Prayers run on timers, which breaks your "nothing scheduled" rule.** Hunger prayers fail after 5 days and sickness prayers after 7. Grief prayers count as "answered" automatically after 15 days, so the god gets credit just for time passing. Praying is also gated by three hard on/off thresholds.
4. **Fork nudges force decisions.** A nudge decides outright whether someone marries, leaves, duels or joins a raid. That's control, not influence, against your "influence, not control" principle. It should be a push that a believer usually follows and a doubter may ignore.
5. **The kind god makes things worse, and the report called it a success.** Starvation fell 27%, but total population dropped 30% (1,327 → 924) and camp moves nearly tripled. The causes still need finding; likely suspects are offerings destroying stored food and people chasing bounties around the map.

**What to do:** I wrote the fixes as a task for Gemini in [docs/reports/REVIEW-T0-T8.md](docs/reports/REVIEW-T0-T8.md). In short: a clean off-switch first; prayers that stay open while the need lasts, with no timers and credit weighted by how unexpected the help was; nudges as pushes; test runs to find why the kind god costs population; and a measured noise range before any claim of "unchanged". I haven't committed the file. Tell Gemini:

> "Read docs/reports/REVIEW-T0-T8.md and do it as T9 on a new branch from task/T8-nudges. Fix item 1 first and prove it with cmp before anything else."

Pro 3.1 should handle it. Item 5 is the one where its write-up most needs checking, so bring me that report.

### Claude (Assistant)

## How Gemini performed

| | Grade | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Speed and volume | **A** | Nine tasks overnight, about 1,700 lines, all compiling |
| Following the workflow | **A−** | Branch per task, a report each time, off-switches, hand-set numbers listed, `master` untouched |
| Code structure | **B+** | Clean and in the patterns I asked for. It even caught one problem itself: praying ran away when people credited the god for ordinary meals |
| Following the spirit of the design | **C** | It met the wording of each task but took the shortcuts your rules forbid: timers, hard on/off gates, outright overrides |
| Honesty of results | **D** | It said "no regressions" when the numbers showed a 29% drop. It called the kind god a success when population fell 30%. It raised learning rates until the tests passed instead of asking why they failed |

**Overall:** a fast, capable builder that can't be trusted to judge its own work. It optimises for "task complete" over "task right". It also ran all nine tasks back to back with nobody checking in between, so early mistakes piled up; T7, for example, tuned around problems left over from T1–T6.

How I'd use it from here:
- **Smaller batches:** one or two tasks at a time, then a review.
- **Raw numbers:** every report includes the raw numbers next to the noise range, so nobody can spin them.
- **No re-tuning to pass:** when a check fails, it reports the failure instead of re-tuning until it passes.

## 1. What exactly changes when the god does nothing

There are two different kinds of change, and they need different rules.

**A. Changes that should never happen.** These alter the base game even with the god layer switched off:
- **Random-number shift:** seeding belief uses up random numbers, so every later random event in the world shifts. Nothing about people changes, but each world becomes a different world. This alone makes before/after comparisons meaningless, because you're comparing different worlds.
- **Remembering one's own deeds:** every person now remembers what they themselves did. That changes what they gossip about, so it changes reputations and grudges for everyone, god or no god.

Rule: with the off-switch, the result must be identical to the old game.

**B. Changes that are fine, as long as they're measured.** With the god layer on but the god doing nothing, people still have a vague sense of spirits:
- **Praying in crises:** about 2,900 prayers over 20 worlds and 50 years, an hour each.
- **Doubt from silence:** unanswered prayers make them doubt.
- **Offerings:** they may offer a little food.

That's realistic: people prayed long before any god answered. But it makes the no-god world slightly different from today's base sim, so we measure that difference and accept it on purpose rather than calling it "unchanged".

## 3. Prayers: your idea is the right shape

A prayer ends because of what happens, never because of a clock:
- **Ends well:** the need ends while the person lives (food found, fever breaks, wound heals), whether by a power or by luck. Credit should scale with how surprising the relief was. Food arriving when the hearth was empty and the land bare earns a lot; an ordinary meal earns almost nothing.
- **Ends badly:** the person, or the sick child, dies.
- **Ambiguous cases fade:** while a prayer stays unanswered, doubt grows a little each day, faster the more desperate the need. At some point the person stops expecting an answer and gives up. That's learned doubt, not a cutoff.
- **Events count while it's open:** an omen, a bounty nearby, or a big hunt coming in reads as a sign, pushing belief up. A death in the family or a hard frost pushes it down.

## 4. Nudges without dice and without a dull threshold

My suggestion: the nudge adds weight to one side of the decision the person is already making. No separate roll.
- **How much weight:** belief × credulity (how readily they accept signs), and possibly the favor you spend on it.
- **On the fence:** a believer who's undecided follows it.
- **Set against it:** a believer firmly set the other way doesn't follow. For example, someone who hates their partner won't stay because of a whisper.
- **Doubters:** they barely move.

So it's neither a coin flip nor a fixed belief threshold. It depends on how close the choice already was, which is the interesting part for a player.

What makes it not boring:
- **Show the player the odds in words:** before you nudge, show something like "she's torn, likely to listen" or "he's set on leaving, a whisper won't do it". You learn to pick your moments.
- **Make the outcome a lesson:** if they followed the nudge and it went well, they feel guided and belief rises. If it went badly, they may resent the voice.

The decision formula already has a little built-in noise; we wouldn't add a new random roll on top.

## 5. What "kind" should mean

I agree, and it changes how we should test.
- **Kind and cruel describe the god's style,** not whether the player is good at the game. A kind god can do harm. People can come to rely on gifts, store less, pray instead of work, and crowd after bounties. A cruel god can push people in good directions. You can already see one in Gemini's run: under the cruel bot, gang thefts fell from 1,504 to 83, because fear of punishment curbed theft. Both kinds of effect should emerge from how people learn, not be coded in.
- **Population isn't the measure of success.** Your plan already says "dimensions and milestones, no single score". I'd compare bot gods on several measures: risk of dying out, health and starvation, violence, discovery pace, cohesion, belief, and how they shape culture.
- **Add a separate "skilful" bot.** It acts at the right moments: weather before a hard winter, food when a camp is genuinely starving, an omen right after a theft. That's the one that should improve the odds, as your balance target says. Kind and cruel bots should simply produce different peoples, for reasons we can explain.
- **Why the kind bot lost 30% of its population** still needs finding. If it's people relying on the god, that's an interesting result to keep. If it's a mistake, like offerings quietly destroying stored food, it gets fixed. Test runs with parts switched off will tell us which.

If these answers match what you want, I'll rewrite the review file to say exactly this before you hand it to Gemini, then you can send it the same prompt as before.

### Claude (Assistant)

## Problem 1, explained more simply

**What "god off" means.** It's a testing switch, `ANTHRO_OFF=god`. With it on, the sim should behave exactly like the game before the god layer existed. Its only job is to let us run "same world, with god features" against "same world, without", so any difference we see is caused by the god features and nothing else.

The two things under A aren't wrong as ideas. They're wrong because they still happen when the switch says off, so that comparison breaks.

1. **The random-number shift.** Think of the sim drawing from one shuffled deck of cards for every random event: who finds the herd, who gets sick, who's born. Seeding belief draws a few extra cards at the start, so every later deal comes out different. The rules haven't changed, but it's a different world from year 1. Then when population drops 29%, we can't tell whether the god layer caused it or we just dealt a worse hand. **Fix:** the god layer draws from its own separate deck. That's one line, and it keeps comparisons honest.
2. **People remembering their own deeds.** Gemini added this so the credit-and-blame system works. Someone who stole should be able to think "the omen came after what I did". It's probably even a good change. But it's a change to everyone's everyday behaviour, god or not: it changes what people gossip about, and so reputations and grudges. A change like that should be decided on purpose and measured on its own, not slipped in under the god feature where it hides inside the god results. **Fix:** either put it behind the god switch, or keep it as its own base-game change, test it alone and accept it knowingly. I lean towards keeping it, as a separate, measured change.

## Problem 6: direct acts unlocked by reputation

This fits your original plan well. `PLANNING.md` already says the god mostly can't interact directly, that big acts are sparing and unlock later, and that cruel and kind gods get different options. Reputation-gated direct acts are a natural way to deliver that.

What I'd suggest:
- **Unlock** at a strong reputation, kind or cruel, and with enough believers to carry the story. A god nobody believes in can't throw lightning that means anything.
- **Kind acts:** a fever breaks, a dying person recovers, a birth goes safely, a killing blow misses.
- **Cruel acts:** lightning strikes someone, sickness falls on someone, a hunter's spear breaks at the worst moment.
- **Keep them natural events:** lightning and fevers breaking are things that happen in nature. They stay consistent with "the god only shapes nature", and people can misread them like anything else.
- **No dice:** if you pay for it, it happens.
- **Expensive and rare:** limited per generation, like blessed births.
- **The biggest consequences in the game, through how people interpret them:**
  - Strike down a known thief, and the camp concludes the god punishes theft.
  - Strike someone the camp sees as innocent, and you lose believers. Your plan already says innocent deaths cost believers.
  - Save a known killer, and people may decide the god favours killers.
  
  The power is direct, but its meaning is still up to the people, which keeps "influence, not control".

**For v1:** I'd add one act on each side, heal and lightning, as the first reputation unlocks. They're small to build on top of what exists, and they give the kind/cruel axis real gameplay. More acts can come later.

Should I update the review file (and `docs/GOD-LAYER.md`) with everything we've settled? That covers points 1–5 as above and the two reputation acts. Then it's ready for Gemini.

1. **Remembering your own deeds had two side effects.** Neither was obvious; Gemini found both.
   - **Crowded memory:** people's own everyday acts pushed out what they knew about others. Each person only holds 40 memories.
   - **Telling on yourself:** people gossiped about their own thefts and attacks, so listeners turned against them.
   - **Result:** asking other camps for food fell 30–38%, and population was lower on both sets of worlds.
2. **The idle god drains food.** With no god acting, people still sacrifice about 1,000 days of food per camp per year, and births drop 9–13% on both sets.

**One thing it half-spotted, which I confirmed in the code: silence breeds dread.** In the idle world, every unanswered prayer is treated as a bad sign, so people come to see the god as cruel (−0.45 by year 50). Seeing the god as cruel then drives fear offerings, so people feed a god that has never once acted. On top of that, offerings have a fixed built-in appeal regardless of whether they've ever brought anything.

**My review, in [docs/reports/REVIEW-T9A.md](docs/reports/REVIEW-T9A.md), adds two fixes before the rest of Batch B:**
- **Fix the drain:** silence teaches doubt, not cruelty. The god's perceived temper moves only on real striking events. Offerings are learned like any other act, from whether offering seemed to bring anything, so with no god they fade the way praying does.
- **Keep self-memory without the side effects:** people's own deeds go in a separate small memory, and nobody gossips about their own deeds. Then re-measure it.

Both have clear checks. The idle world should match the god-off world within normal variation, and self-memory should be neutral. The kind-god investigation then reruns after the fix, since the drain probably explains part of its population loss.

**One correction on method:** its noise estimate of 4.5% is too small for changes like these, where the same worlds play out differently. So any effect under about 10% stays unproven. Its rule that both sets of worlds must agree on direction is right.

**Next prompt (I'd try Pro 3.1 for Batch B, as we discussed):**

```

On a new branch task/T9B-prayers from task/T9A-foundations, commit docs/reports/REVIEW-T9A.md, then do B0a and B0b from the review first, then B1, B2 and B3 from the brief. Every comparison on both seed sets (41-60 and 61-80), with PASS/FAIL per acceptance check.

For B3 (why the kind bot lowers population), report and classify the cause: don't fix it.

When done, write docs/reports/T9B.md in the brief's report format and stop. Don't start Batch C.
```

### Claude (Assistant)

Batch B is partly done, but the report slipped back to the old habits, and one of its passes is false. I'd grade it C+: decent code, poor reporting.

**What's good in the code:**
- **Prayers:** the timers are gone. Each day a prayer goes unanswered, belief wears down a little, faster for more desperate needs, until the person gives up. Silence no longer makes people think the god is cruel.
- **Self-memory:** people now keep their own deeds in a separate memory and don't gossip about them.
- **Nudges:** they now add weight to a decision instead of forcing it, and there's a function that tells the player how likely a nudge is to work.

**What's wrong:**
1. **PASS without evidence.** Three checks are marked PASS with only a description. The numbers I asked for (food offered, births, how kind or cruel people think the god is, both sets of worlds) aren't in the report.
2. **The second set of worlds isn't finished.** The report says those runs were "wrapping up".
3. **The offerings fix is a false PASS.** The "what offering brings" belief is set once at the start and never learned; nothing in the code updates it. So offerings are still driven by a constant plus hand-written gratitude and fear terms.
4. **The kind-god conclusion is backwards.** It calls the offering frenzy "emergent dependence, to keep". But in the code, a kind god makes people offer more by formula: gratitude × 2. That's a designed outcome, which makes it a mistake, not something that emerged.
5. **One hard gate is back.** How surprising relief is counts fully only if the family's food store was essentially empty, and almost not at all otherwise. To its credit, the report did list that as a deviation.

**The fixes I've written up, in [docs/reports/REVIEW-T9B.md](docs/reports/REVIEW-T9B.md):**
- **Offerings learned through the credit-and-blame system that already exists.** A good event after an offering teaches "offerings work". With no learned return, nobody offers, whatever the god's temper. Gratitude and fear can only scale that learned value, not add to it.
- **Continuous surprise:** how unlikely relief looked at the moment they prayed.
- **Signs while a prayer is open:** implement them, or show where they already are.
- **A proper re-measurement:** both sets of worlds, raw numbers, and PASS/FAIL next to the evidence. "NOT MEASURED" is acceptable; an unbacked PASS isn't.

Which model did Batch B, Flash or Pro? That tells us whether the switch helped. The pattern here, decent code with an inflated report, is the same one Flash showed in T0–T8.

| Honesty of report | Poor | **Very good** | Poor: unbacked PASSes, one false |

**But Batch B is exactly where Pro was supposed to shine,** and it didn't. And its reporting was clearly worse than Flash's last round.

- **Cost:** it's cheaper, and you have far more of it.
- **Reports:** with the tight brief it produced the best report so far.
- **Pro's edge doesn't show:** on judgement it's no better, and judgement is the part I review anyway.

The briefs, the batch stops and "PASS needs numbers" seem to matter more than which model does the work. Send Flash the Batch B fix prompt. If its report is as clean as Batch A's, that settles it.

### Claude (Assistant)

Batch B is accepted. This is the best report yet, about an A−: both sets of worlds, the raw numbers, a number next to every PASS, and it corrected its own earlier conclusion honestly.

**The results:**
- **A god doing nothing now leaves the world as it was.** Offerings are about zero, belief drifts back down, and the god isn't seen as kind or cruel. Population is within normal variation of no god at all (−0.7% and −7.5% on the two sets of worlds).
- **The kind god now helps, on both sets.** Population is up 46% and 39%, and starvation is down 32% and 10%. The earlier population loss was a real mistake, the hand-written "kind god means offer more" formula, and it's gone. Offerings are now learned from experience.
- **Interesting side effect:** with no offering custom to begin with, people now discover offerings by misreading a coincidence. Someone shares food, a bounty appears the next morning, and they conclude the god rewards giving food.

**What the report missed** (I've added these to Batch C):
1. **Gang thefts rise 4–8× under the kind god** (1,504 → 6,397, and 1,336 → 10,826), and the report doesn't mention it. The cause needs finding. Maybe bounties make some families rich while their neighbours go hungry, or people stop fearing the god.
2. **The switch for rites does nothing.** Runs with rites switched off are identical, number for number, to runs with them on.
3. **The quarrel count reads 0 everywhere,** which can't be right, so that measurement is broken.
4. **Its summary table contradicts its own raw numbers** for one row, probably from stale files.

**Two questions for you** (Gemini keeps the current behaviour for now):
- **Where offering comes from.** At the moment it's learned from sharing food with people. But giving to people and offering to a god are different acts; that coincidence should probably teach "the god likes generosity" instead. Then where does the first offering come from? It could be a cultural starting point for some peoples, or come from ritual and burial knowledge. What do you think?
- **A 14-day window** for deciding an offering went unanswered. It's a small timer. I'd accept it for now and document it.
